coordinator · Research baby-science facts for lyrics11:17 PM

You are researching primary scientific literature for a fact sheet that will feed a dense, fact-packed song about infant science (think Hamilton-style lyrical density). Deliverable: return your findings as your final message in Markdown (do NOT write any files). Use WebSearch and WebFetch (load them via ToolSearch "select:WebSearch,WebFetch" first).

Themes you own: MOVEMENT, LANGUAGE, COGNITION. For each fact, give: one or two lines, the specific number(s), the primary paper (first author, year, journal) or best authoritative source, a URL, and a reliability flag: [solid] / [contested] / [pop-science-only]. Prefer primary papers and university press releases over blogs. Verify numbers; do not invent them. If you cannot verify a number, say so explicitly.

Items to verify and quantify (add others you find striking):

MOVEMENT

  • Karen Adolph (NYU): Adolph et al. 2012 Psychological Science "How do you learn to walk? Thousands of steps and dozens of falls per day" - 12-19-month-olds average 2,368 steps/hour, 17 falls/hour, ~14,000 steps/day, distance of ~46 football fields/day (check exact figures: 701 m/hour?); also Adolph's visual cliff / slopes work (infants' knowledge doesn't transfer from crawling to walking: Adolph 1997 Monographs; Kretch & Adolph 2013 "Cliff or step?"); Adolph's "learning to crawl" and crawling styles (belly, hands-knees, bear crawl, bum shuffle, 25 variants); Adolph & Robinson; Netflix Babies episode "First Steps"/"Movement" - was Adolph featured? Check NYU press / her lab news page (adolphlab).
  • Eric Walle (UC Merced): Walle & Campos 2014 Developmental Psychology "Infant language development is related to the acquisition of walking" - walkers had bigger receptive and productive vocabularies than same-age crawlers; UC Merced press release Feb 2020 about Netflix "Babies" (which episode? "First Steps"?). Also Walle's follow-up in Chinese infants (He, Walle & Campos 2015 Infancy) replicating.
  • Libby Cowgill (University of Missouri): bone shape / femur cross-sectional geometry and locomotion (Cowgill et al. 2010 AJPA "Waddling and toddling: the biomechanical effects of an immature gait"), her Netflix Babies appearance (Missouri press release, which episode: "Crawling"? "Movement"?). What was measured?
  • Reflexes: Moro, rooting, sucking, stepping (disappears ~2 months; Thelen's leg-weight explanation: Thelen, Fisher & Ridley-Johnson 1984), palmar grasp (can support own weight? - a 1891 Robinson claim; flag reliability), Babinski, tonic neck; when each disappears.
  • Motor milestones: WHO Multicentre Growth Reference Study 2006 windows (sitting without support median 6.0 mo, 3.8-9.2; hands-and-knees crawling 8.5 mo, 5.2-13.5; walking alone 12.1 mo, 8.2-17.6); ~ what % of babies skip crawling.
  • Thelen's treadmill stepping; "motor development is not maturation alone" (dynamic systems).
  • Back to Sleep campaign delayed motor milestones slightly (Davis 1998 Arch Pediatr Adolesc Med) and reduced SIDS by ~50%.
  • Culture: Kipsigis in Kenya walk earlier (Super 1976); swaddling/ cradleboard (Hopi, Dennis 1940) - walking unaffected.
  • Sleep twitches and motor map (Blumberg).
  • Fine motor: pincer grasp ~9-10 months.

LANGUAGE

  • Patricia Kuhl (U Washington): perceptual narrowing - 6-8-month-olds discriminate non-native phonemes, 10-12-month-olds don't (Werker & Tees 1984 Infant Behav Dev - Hindi/Thompson Salish contrasts); Kuhl 2003 PNAS: 9-month-olds learned Mandarin phonetics from live tutors after 12 sessions (~5 hours) but NOT from video/audio; Kuhl's "social gating"; Kuhl 1992 Science "perceptual magnet" at 6 months. Was Kuhl in Netflix Babies "First Words"?
  • Statistical learning: Saffran, Aslin & Newport 1996 Science - 8-month-olds segment words from 2 minutes of continuous syllable stream (24 infants).
  • Infant-directed speech / parentese: Fernald; Ramírez-Esparza, García-Sierra & Kuhl 2014 Developmental Science (parentese in one-on-one context predicts vocabulary: infants hearing most had 433 words at 24 mo vs 169 for least? verify); Ferjan Ramírez 2020 PNAS parentese coaching RCT.
  • Word gap: Hart & Risley 1995 (30 million word gap by age 3; 42 families) and its criticisms (Sperry, Sperry & Miller 2019 Child Development failed to replicate; Gilkerson 2017 LENA estimate ~4 million words gap); flag contested.
  • Baby Einstein / DVDs: Zimmerman, Christakis & Meltzoff 2007 J Pediatrics - each hour/day of baby DVDs associated with 6-8 fewer vocabulary words in 8-16-month-olds; Disney refund 2009; DeLoache 2010 Psychological Science (no learning from video). Flag.
  • Milestones: babbling ~6 months (canonical babbling), first words ~12 months (range), 50-word mark ~18 months, vocabulary spurt ~18 months (is it real? Ganger & Brent 2004 contested), 200-300 words at 24 mo; words understood vs produced (comprehend ~50 words at 12 mo).
  • Pointing: emerges ~12 months (Tomasello, Carpenter & Liszkowski 2007; Liszkowski declarative pointing); pointing predicts vocabulary (Colonnesi 2010 meta-analysis).
  • Newborn cry melody & language (Mampe 2009) - only if not covered elsewhere; skip detail.
  • Bilingual infants: Kovács & Mehler 2009 PNAS 7-month-olds; Byers-Heinlich 2010 Psychological Science newborns of bilingual mothers.
  • Gesture / sign claims (baby sign language doesn't boost language: Kirk et al. 2013 Child Development) - flag.
  • Eric Walle's walking-vocabulary link (brief cross-ref).
  • Fast mapping (Carey & Bartlett 1978 "chromium").
  • Fernald, Marchman & Weisleder 2013 Developmental Science: SES differences in processing speed by 18 months, 6-month gap by 24 months.

COGNITION

  • Object permanence: Piaget ~8 months (A-not-B error 8-12 months); Baillargeon 1985/1987 Cognition "drawbridge" study: 3.5-4.5-month-olds look longer at impossible events; violation-of-expectation; Baillargeon's "core knowledge" of physics (support, containment).
  • Elizabeth Spelke: core knowledge; Spelke 1992 Psychological Review; Xu & Spelke 2000 Cognition: 6-month-olds discriminate 8 vs 16 dots but not 8 vs 12 (1:2 ratio; 9-month-olds do 2:3); Wynn 1992 Nature "Addition and subtraction by human infants" (5-month-olds, 1+1=2 vs 1+1=1, Mickey Mouse dolls); Izard 2009 PNAS newborns match number across senses (4 vs 12).
  • Fei Xu: Xu & Garcia 2008 PNAS - 8-month-olds infer population from sample (ping-pong balls, red/white); Gweon, Tenenbaum & Schulz 2010 PNAS 15-month-olds; was Xu in Netflix Babies "What Babies Know"?
  • Celeste Kidd: Goldilocks effect - Kidd, Piantadosi & Aslin 2012 PLoS ONE: 7-8-month-olds look away from events that are too predictable or too surprising (U-shaped); 2014 follow-up with sounds.
  • Rebecca Saxe (MIT): awake infant fMRI - Deen et al. 2017 Nature Communications: 4-6-month-olds' visual cortex already shows face- and scene-preferring regions (9 infants scanned, out of 17?); Saxe scanned her own son (Smithsonian 2015); was Saxe in Netflix Babies?
  • Susan Hespos (Northwestern): infants' knowledge of liquids vs solids (Hespos, Ferry & Rips 2009 Psychological Science: 5-month-olds); Hespos & Baillargeon containment; was Hespos in Netflix Babies?
  • Memory: infant memory (Rovee-Collier mobile conjugate reinforcement: 3-month-olds remember 1 week, 6-month-olds 2 weeks); infantile amnesia (earliest memories ~3.5 years; Bauer); deferred imitation (Meltzoff 1988: 9-month-olds remember 24h; 14-month-olds remember 1 week).
  • Mirror self-recognition at ~18-24 months (Amsterdam 1972; Lewis & Brooks-Gunn): rouge test, ~65% at 20-24 months; cross-cultural variation (Kenyan children fail at 6 years: Broesch 2011).
  • Habituation and looking-time method basics (Fantz 1961/1963).
  • Neonatal imitation (Meltzoff & Moore 1977 Science tongue protrusion) and the failed replication (Oostenbroek 2016 Current Biology, 106 infants) - flag contested.
  • Executive function / delayed gratification in toddlers (Mischel marshmallow at 4 yrs; Watts 2018 replication halved effect) - brief.
  • "Babies are born scientists" - Gopnik's framing; Stahl & Feigenson 2015 Science: 11-month-olds explore and learn more about objects that violated expectations (e.g., a ball passing through a wall).
  • Categorization and causal learning in infants (Gopnik/ Schulz blicket detector, 2-4-year-olds).

Return a dense, well-organized Markdown list grouped under MOVEMENT / LANGUAGE / COGNITION, ~150-200 lines, each fact with number + citation + URL + reliability flag. Note explicitly which Netflix "Babies" scientists you confirmed via press releases or interviews (quote the episode name if found).

The search confirmed the study and its headline figures.

- **Publication:** Adolph and colleagues published it in *Psychological Science* in 2012, vol. 23(11), pp. 1387–1394 (Adolph, K., Cole, W., Komati, M., Garciaguirre, J., Badaly, D., Lingeman, J., Chan, G., and Sotsky, R. (2012). How Do You Learn to Walk? Thousands of Steps and Dozens of Falls per Day. Psychological Science, 23 (11), 1387-1394).
- **Headline figures:** The abstract reports that 12- to 19-month-olds averaged 2,368 steps and 17 falls per hour (1-4).
- **Sample and method:** The British Psychological Society digest says the team videoed 151 infants aged 11 to 19 months during free play (2-2). Recordings ran 15 to 60 minutes (10-2).
- **Walking vs. crawling:** Novice walkers covered more ground faster than expert crawlers but fell at a similar rate, which the authors read as a motive for switching to walking (1-5).
- **Bouts:** Walking happened in short, variable bouts rather than steady gait (1-7).
- **Caveat:** A secondary blog post notes the data came from indoor free play in one narrow age band, so it should not be read as a universal rule (6-9, 6-10).

The search returned no independent replication, so treat these as the findings of a single study.

The 2014 paper is Walle and Campos, "Infant language development is related to the acquisition of walking," in *Developmental Psychology* 50, 336–348 (Walle, E. A. and Campos, J. J. (2014). Infant language development is related to the acquisition of walking. Developmental Psychology, 50, 336-348.).

- **Design:** It was a longitudinal study of infants aged 10 to 13.5 months. Parents completed an online questionnaire every two weeks over three months (A longitudinal study by Walle and Campos (2014) explored the relationship between walking onset and language acquisition among 10 to 13.5 month-old infants. Parents were asked to report infant language and locomotor development consecutively every two weeks, over a 3-month period through an online questionnaire.). Vocabulary was measured with a checklist of words infants understood and produced (parents recalled which words their infants understood, receptively and productively).
- **Main finding:** Walking onset was followed by a sharp rise in vocabulary. The authors reported that the spike was tied to walking rather than to age (The two studies together showed that the spike in language was clearly associated with walking — not with age.).
- **Cross-cultural follow-up:** A related study with Chinese infants (He, Walle & Campos, 2015, *Infancy*) examined the same link across countries (He, M., Walle, E. A., & Campos, J. J. (2015). A cross-national investigation of the relation between infant walking and language development. Infancy, 20, 283-305.).
- **Limitations:** Critics note that the study relied only on parental questionnaires for both language and motor measures (One limitation within this study was the use of parental questionnaires to solely determine both language and locomotor measures.). Walle and Campos also did not test the mechanism behind the link (the only longitudinal study to investigate the association between infant walking and language (Walle and Campos, 2014) did not explore potential processes that may account for this link.).
- **Later work:** A follow-up with 13-month-olds found no vocabulary advantage for walkers over crawlers (Thirteen-month-old crawlers knew as many words as 13-month-old walkers.). Walle has also suggested that parents who encourage object play may narrow the gap (When parents are encouraging their child to interact with objects more frequently, their crawling infants have a vocabulary that looks more like that of walking infants). A Purdue poster on toy-carrying during walking bouts builds on the same idea (Given that independent walking provides infants with unique opportunities to walk while carrying toys, these toy carriage walking bouts might be key to this observed increase in word learning.).

The WHO Multicentre Growth Reference Study windows of achievement come from a 2006 Acta Paediatrica supplement, based on longitudinal data from 816 children aged 4 to 24 months in five countries. The study's narrowest window is sitting without support, at 5.4 months. The widest are walking alone (9.4 months) and standing alone (10.0 months). Failure time models set each window between the 1st and 99th percentiles.

Key percentile bounds (in months, 1st to 99th) from the same paper:
- Sitting without support: 3.8 to 9.2
- Hands-and-knees crawling: 5.2 to 13.5
- Standing alone: 6.9 to 16.9

The search snippet cut off before the walking-alone bounds, so I couldn't confirm those. Hands-and-knees crawling was absent in about 4.3% of children. The study's own criterion for sitting without support requires the child to hold the head erect for at least 10 seconds without using arms or hands for balance. Walking alone requires at least five independent upright steps with no contact. Those criteria come from a secondary thesis appendix, not the primary paper.

The WHO's official motor milestone page links to the tables, graphs, and the 2006 paper.

Links: [{"title":"Modelling of infant stepping refex and physical growth","url":"https://www.epfl.ch/labs/biorob/students/past/page-156481-en-html"},{"title":"Postnatally, the stepping response can be elicited right after birth, but soon diminishes","url":"https://www.thieme-connect.com/products/ejournals/html/10.1055/s-2000-7459"},{"title":"Neuropediatrics 2000; 31(4): 180-185","url":"https://www.thieme-connect.de/products/ejournals/pdf/10.1055/s-2000-7459.pdf"},{"title":"Jump to content","url":"https://wiki.ubc.ca/Course:KIN355/2020_Projects/Stepping_Reflex"},{"title":"780bec 0842f18b5836457ab745ba641fb04213","url":"https://nyuactionlab.com/_files/ugd/780bec_0842f18b5836457ab745ba641fb04213.pdf?index=true"},{"title":"1. Introduction","url":"https://0-www-mdpi-com.brum.beds.ac.uk/2227-9032/9/2/109"},{"title":"Treadmill training in early infancy: sensory and motor effects","url":"https://musculoskeletalkey.com/treadmill-training-in-early-infancy-sensory-and-motor-effects/"},{"title":"1. Introduction","url":"https://www.mdpi.com/1424-8220/15/8/19006/html"},{"title":"Stepping response","url":"https://www.lancaster.ac.uk/fas/psych/glossary/infantile_responses/stepping_response"}]

The search didn't return the 1984 paper itself, but several secondary sources describe its findings. The citation in the Neuropediatrics reference list is Thelen E, Fisher DM, Ridley-Johnson R. The relationship between physical growth and a newborn reflex. Infants Behavior Dev. 1984; 7 479-493.

- **Core claim:** Thelen's group argued that the stepping reflex is not lost through cortical maturation. Thelen and Fisher proposed that the normal disappearance of the reflex was due to asymmetry in weight gained and increased muscle strength during infant development, leading to disappearance of the behavior when the legs became too heavy to lift with the current strength level.
- **Leg-weight experiment:** Stepping could be inhibited if small weights were added to the infant's legs, and infants who had stopped stepping could be made to step again if their heavy legs were buoyed in a tank of water (Thelen et al., 1984). A study summary also reports that masses were added to the infants' legs, which led to a decrease in stepping amplitude and rate.
- **Limits of the evidence:** One review notes that no one has directly tested the relationship between infant leg strength and weight due to difficulties in experimental design. Another source says it is still not clear if the disappearance of the response is caused by gradual maturation of the central nervous system, environmental factors, or biomechanical factors such as added body weight and/or increased muscle mass of the legs.

The study you're looking for appears to be published in *Pediatrics*, not the *Archives of Pediatrics & Adolescent Medicine*. Davis, Moon, Sachs, and Ottolini, "Effects of Sleep Position on Infant Motor Development," *Pediatrics* 102(5), 1998, pp. 1135-1140. The Archives paper from that period is Jantz and colleagues (1997), which studied motor milestones after the sleep-position change.

**Findings of the Davis 1998 study:**
- The team tracked 351 healthy term infants, recruited before 2 months of age. Parents logged sleep position and awake time spent prone until 6 months. A developmental log tracked milestones until walking. [9-10, 9-5, 9-7, 9-8]
- Prone sleepers reached several milestones earlier than supine sleepers, including rolling, tripod sitting, creeping, crawling, and pulling to stand. Walking age did not differ significantly. [9-11, 9-12, 9-13]
- All infants reached every milestone within the accepted normal range, and the authors concluded the difference is not a reason to abandon the AAP's sleep-position recommendations. [9-16, 9-18]

**Context:** A 2010s review notes that the decline in prone sleeping was accompanied by delayed gross motor development, partly because parents following Back-to-Sleep also avoided prone play while awake. [4-3] The AAP continues to recommend supervised "tummy time" while infants are awake. [3-6]

Links: [{"title":"Children have no fear of hazards when learning to walk, finds study","url":"https://nurseryworld.co.uk/news/article/children-have-no-fear-of-hazards-when-learning-to-walk-finds-study"},{"title":"Babies on the Edge","url":"https://www.scientificamerican.com/article/babies-on-the-edge/"},{"title":"From the Society for Research in Child Development press release via EurekAlert!:","url":"https://therapytoronto.ca/news/?p=5780"},{"title":"From the Society for Research in Child Development press release via EurekAlert!:","url":"https://therapytoronto.ca/news/2012/08/study-suggests-infants-avoidance-of-drop-off-reflects-specific-motor-ability-not-fear/"},{"title":"sites.usc.edu","url":"https://sites.usc.edu/ldrlab/?p=2017"},{"title":"Cliff or Step? Posture‐Specific Learning at the Edge of a Drop‐Off","url":"https://scinapse.io/papers/2159160623"},{"title":"profiles.sc-ctsi.org","url":"https://profiles.sc-ctsi.org/kari.kretch"},{"title":"\"For each new phase of motor development, infants have to relearn how to keep themselves safe.\"","url":"https://exchangepress.com/eed/issue/3043"},{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/4357016"}]

The search turned up the 2013 paper itself: Kretch and Adolph, "Cliff or Step? Posture-Specific Learning at the Edge of a Drop-Off," *Child Development*, vol. 84, issue 1, pp. 226–240 (Cliff or step? Posture-specific learning at the edge of a drop-off.). The abstract is only partly visible in the results. It says an actual, adjustable drop-off apparatus was used to investigate whether learning to distinguish a step from a cliff transfers from crawling to walking.

Press coverage of the study reports these findings:
- Experienced crawlers tried to crawl down safe drop-offs within their abilities and refused to crawl down drop-offs that were too large relative to their abilities.
- Novice walkers attempted to walk down impossibly large drop-offs, even the 90-cm cliff.
- Experienced walkers did go down using alternative strategies, indicating that they weren't afraid of the drop-off.
- The authors concluded that the learning is specific to posture. Coverage says it suggests that what infants learn is to perceive the limits of their ability to crawl or walk, not a generalized fear of heights.

The sample was roughly 50 infants, with 12-month-old experienced crawlers, 12-month-old novice walkers, and 18-month-old experienced walkers. The apparatus had a maximum height of 90 cm. The results are from press releases and news coverage, not the full paper, so I could not confirm the exact statistics from the article text.

Links: [{"title":"Blumberg Curr Opin Physiol 2020","url":"https://blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/sites/blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/files/2022-01/Blumberg_Curr_Opin_Physiol_2020.pdf"},{"title":"blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu","url":"https://blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/node/36"},{"title":"Blumberg Current Biology 2013","url":"https://blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/sites/blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/files/2022-01/Blumberg_Current_Biology_2013.pdf"},{"title":"blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu","url":"https://blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/sites/blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/files/2026-04/zsaf410.pdf"},{"title":"blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu","url":"https://blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/sites/blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/files/2022-11/Sokoloff.Current%20Biology.2021.pdf"},{"title":"Mark S. Blumberg, PhD","url":"https://neuroscience.grad.uiowa.edu/node/521"},{"title":"Blumberg.Current%20Biology.2013 2","url":"https://blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/sites/blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/files/2022-11/Blumberg.Current%20Biology.2013_2.pdf"},{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4414046"},{"title":"Sleep Twitches Shape Babies' Growing Brains","url":"https://www.brainfacts.org/thinking-sensing-and-behaving/sleep/2021/sleep-twitches-shape-babies-growing-brains-011521"}]

The search turned up a body of work from Mark Blumberg's lab at the University of Iowa on infant twitching during sleep.

- **Twitches as active-sleep markers:** Twitching is common in infant mammals during active sleep, and it is seen across many species. Twitches are not goal-directed movements, and they were once dismissed as byproducts of dreaming. The lab's view is that they are functional, since they trigger activity in somatosensory cortex and in sensorimotor structures across the nervous system (twitching is a ubiquitous feature of active sleep in infant mammals; twitches robustly, reliably, and somatotopically trigger bursts of activity in somato-sensory cortex).
- **Link to sensorimotor maps:** Researchers describe twitches as particularly abundant in early infancy when critical sensorimotor networks and topographic maps are established. The lab argues they may support development, maintenance, and repair of the sensorimotor system (twitches are critical for the development and maintenance of the sensorimotor system). That is an argument from the lab's own program, and the search results don't show it as settled consensus.
- **Structure of twitching:** A 2013 study in rat pups found within- and between-limb synergies at multiple time-scales; these synergies exist at birth and are modified lawfully across the early postnatal period.
- **Twitching outside REM:** A 2021 study in human infants reported that beginning around 3 months of age in human infants, twitches emerge unexpectedly during quiet sleep. These twitches are synchronized with sleep spindles, thus implicating them in sensorimotor plasticity. A 2026 study in 6-month-old infants found high-intensity twitching during N2 and REM but not N3 sleep.
- **Possible clinical use:** Blumberg has suggested that twitching could be studied to parse out sleep abnormalities hinting at neurodevelopmental disorders to come, like autism spectrum disorder and cerebral palsy. This is still a research direction, not an established diagnostic tool.

Searching once turned up good corroboration for the 1891 paper, mostly from secondary sources.

- **Attribution and date:** A Lancaster University psychology glossary credits Louis Robinson, whom it calls an avid Darwinist, with first describing in 1891 that newborns could hold their own weight on a horizontal rod. It cites his "Darwinism in the nursery" for this. (It was the avid Darwinist Louis Robinson (1857-????) who first described in 1891 that the human newborn was able to support its own weight when holding on to a horizontal rod.)
- **Method and results:** The same glossary says Testing 60 infants under one month-of-age, he reported they could maintain the grasp for at least 10 seconds, with one infant doing so for 2 minutes and 35 seconds. A Drummond text quoting Robinson describes the test as extending a finger or walking stick to the infant and says that in at least half of these the experiment was tried within an hour of birth.
- **Contemporary reception:** A 1891 *Review of Reviews* abstract says the paper reports experiments on infants under a month old, and that the grasp gave strong support to the 'Darwinian theory of our Simian origin'.
- **Primary text:** A Wikisource reprint of Robinson's piece appears in the *Popular Science Monthly* (March 1892), which I did not read in full.

One caveat: I didn't see the original 1891 *Nineteenth Century* text, so the figures above come from secondary summaries. The 2 minutes 35 seconds figure and the "60 infants" count are the most specific details, and they should be checked against the original.

The paper is Cowgill, Warrener, Pontzer and Ocobock (2010), "Waddling and toddling: the biomechanical effects of an immature gait," American Journal of Physical Anthropology 143(1): 52–61 (DOI 10.1002/ajpa.21289).

- **Femoral shape:** The authors describe the child femur as changing from a roughly round tube into a teardrop-shaped shaft with a posterior ridge, which they tie to the loading differences between immature and mature walkers. Femoral shape changes during the course of human growth, transitioning from a subcircular tube to a teardrop-shaped diaphysis with a posterior pilaster.
- **Ground reaction forces:** The study tested whether young and adult walkers differ in ground reaction forces. Its result was that the mediolateral forces are higher in younger groups, which the authors connect to the child femur's reinforcement in that plane. The results of this analysis indicate that GRFs differ between young walkers and adults in that normalized mediolateral (ML) forces are significantly higher in younger age groups. In addition, these differences between children and adults in the relative level of ML bending force are reflected in changes in femoral geometry during growth.
- **Proposed causes:** The authors attribute the gait differences partly to body build. Their explanation is a small bicondylar angle and a relatively broad body in young children, which the search summaries state as a minimal bicondylar angle and relatively broad body in young children.
- **Earlier version:** A 2009 conference abstract under the same title, by Wittman, Cowgill, Pontzer and Ocobock, appeared in the AJPA meeting supplement (78th AAPA meeting, Chicago), pp. 93.

I did not access the full text, so the summaries above come from the abstract and indexed excerpts. The search results did not include the paper's specific figures or sample details.

**Scientists named in Episode 3 "Crawling":**

1. **Dr. Marianne Barbu-Roth**, director of the Motor Development Group, Université of Paris (France)
   - Showed newborns can move from the mother's stomach to her breast after one hour, and can crawl or swim in water soon after birth.
   - Used a black-dot illusion on a white background. Babies held above the dots began to "swim in the air," which she presented as evidence of a link between vision, the brain, and the motor system.
   - Built the "Crawlerskate," a modified skateboard that let babies about two days old move across a mattress without supporting their heads.

2. **Dr. Michelle Lampl**, professor, Emory Center for the Study of Human Health, Atlanta
   - Measured babies' length over two days, with readings varying by two centimeters.
   - Measured thirty babies daily and found growth of 0.5 to 1.65 cm in a single day, separated by two to twenty-eight days of no growth. This supports growth in jumps rather than a smooth curve.

3. **Dr. Karen Adolph**, psychology professor, NYU (New York City)
   - Used an "adjustable slope apparatus" that increased in steepness by six degrees at a time.
   - Found novice crawlers approached a 90-degree drop-off the same way as a shallow slope.
   - Found intermediate crawlers stopped at the edge, while advanced crawlers (about twenty weeks of crawling experience) used alternative strategies, such as going backward down the slope.

4. **Dr. Jacalyn Duffin** (cited in the review, not in the episode summary)
   - Author of *Clio in the Clinic*. The review says she cites multiple studies suggesting that placing babies on their backs to prevent SIDS may have changed their crawling and rolling timelines. The page does not state her affiliation.

The search didn't return the 1997 monograph itself, so these findings come from later papers that describe it.

- **Slope study:** Adolph's 1997 longitudinal work is described as showing that learning to avoid impossibly steep slopes does not transfer across developmental changes from crawling to walking postures.
- **Crawling phase:** Infants whose first weeks of crawling were tracked plunged headfirst down impossibly steep slopes on an adjustable walkway, but their judgments grew more accurate over the following weeks (became increasingly accurate).
- **Walking phase:** When the same infants began walking, they tried to descend the slopes they had recently avoided while crawling. A review says new walkers showed no transfer from their old, familiar crawling posture to their new, upright walking posture.
- **Learning rate:** Errors dropped during walking, but a review notes that learning was no faster the second time around.
- **Interpretation:** Adolph's account treats each posture as its own problem space. One review puts it this way: learning to learn in an earlier developing posture does not transfer to a later-developing posture.
- **Caveat on scope:** A 2009 review notes that walkers' responses eventually become increasingly adaptive with everyday experience, which means the lack of transfer is about the start of each new posture rather than a permanent deficit.

Hidden skills : a dynamic systems analysis of treadmill stepping during the first year / Esther Thelen, Beverly D. Ulrich.
- **Dynamic-systems framing:** A 2006 *Child Development* tribute by Spencer and colleagues describes how Thelen's work on newborn stepping and kicking challenged the view that brain maturation alone drives motor development. The puzzle was that newborn stepping disappeared within the first 3 months, whereas kicking continued and increased in frequency.
- **Body-composition explanation:** A later review notes that developmental changes in the ratio of leg fat to muscle over infants' first year of life were responsible for the famous U-shaped developmental trajectory in infants' upright stepping movements.
- **Treadmill method:** A table of contents for a dynamic-systems book includes a chapter on Entraining the Intrinsic Dynamics to the Environment: Treadmill-elicited Stepping in Infants, which covers the treadmill paradigm.

The search returned the StatPearls entry, but it covers only some of the reflexes you asked about, and other sources give different ages.

**StatPearls (the only result from the source you named):** It gives onset and disappearance ages for several reflexes. The asymmetric tonic neck reflex starts at 35 weeks gestation and is gone by three months. Moro develops by 28 weeks gestation and disappears by six months. The grasp reflex follows the same timeline, also developing by 28 weeks and disappearing by six months. Rooting begins at 32 weeks gestation and decreases after one month. The StatPearls excerpt gives no disappearance age for Babinski and does not cover stepping.

**Other sources, for comparison:**
- **Stepping:** Estimates cluster around 2–3 months. One source says it disappears around 2-3 months, and another says it disappears at about 2 months.
- **Babinski:** Estimates range from about 9 to 24 months. One source puts it at about 9-12 months. Another says it disappears by 1-2 years. A nursing-exam page claims a positive sign is normal up to 12 to 24 months of age, but that is a low-quality source.
- **Palmar grasp:** One source says it diminishes by 3 months and disappears by 12 months old, which conflicts with the StatPearls 6-month figure.
- **Moro:** A Queensland child health guide says it diminishes in strength by 3–4 months and disappears by 6 months.

**Caveat:** Ages differ across sources, and the grasp and Babinski figures in particular vary widely. For clinical use, check a current primary reference or the full StatPearls article, which I could not access directly.

The search confirmed the core design of the 1996 study, but none of the results mention the sample size of 24 infants, so I can't verify that figure from these sources.

- **Design and result:** One source says the study tested whether 8-month-olds could segment words from a continuous, cue-free speech stream. The infants were exposed for roughly two minutes, and the authors reported that they picked up word boundaries from the statistical relationships between neighboring sounds (the word segmentation was accomplished by 8-month-old infants based solely on the statistical relationships between neighboring speech sounds; this word segmentation was based on statistical learning from only 2 minutes of exposure).
- **Stimuli:** The speech was built from four three-syllable nonsense words, presented in random order with no pauses or pitch cues (the infants heard a continuous stream of speech sounds comprising four randomly ordered three-syllable nonsense "words," with no pauses between the words).
- **Test:** Infants were tested on words versus part-words that spanned word boundaries, and their looking responses differed between them (a postfamiliarization test compared the infants' responses to words versus part-words).
- **Terminology:** Saffran and colleagues introduced the term "statistical learning" for this process of acquiring information about how elements in the input are distributed (the term statistical learning to refer to the process by which learners acquire information about distributions of elements in the input).
- **Caveat on the sample:** I could not find the participant count in these results. A follow-up study by Saffran's group used 3-minute exposure, so don't conflate it with the 1996 2-minute design.

- **Publication:** The paper is Ramírez-Esparza, García-Sierra and Kuhl (Developmental Science, 2014, 17(6), 880–891, doi 10.1111/desc.12172). It is titled "Look who's talking: speech style and social context in language input to infants are linked to concurrent and future speech development."

- **Key findings:** Parentese speech in 1:1 contexts is positively correlated with both concurrent speech and later word production. Mediation analyses further show that the effect of parentese speech-1:1 on infants' later language is mediated by concurrent speech.

The 2007 study by Zimmerman, Christakis, and Meltzoff found a link between baby DVD viewing and smaller vocabularies in infants aged 8 to 16 months. Each daily hour of viewing was associated with about six to eight fewer words understood, compared with infants who did not watch. Coverage of the findings consistently reports this figure, and the sources below are news and university pieces rather than the paper itself.

- **Age-specific results:** Older toddlers, aged 17 to 24 months, showed neither harm nor benefit from the videos (The baby DVDs/videos had no positive or negative effect on the vocabulary of toddlers ages 17 to 24 months.).
- **Method:** Researchers used parent telephone interviews with families in Minnesota and Washington, asking parents how many words their child understood from a list (Zimmerman and colleagues conducted random telephone interviews with more than 1,000 families in Minnesota and Washington with babies and asked detailed questions about television and video viewing. Parents of the 8- to 16-month-olds were asked how many words like "choo-choo," "mommy" and "nose" their child understood.).
- **Authors' interpretation:** Meltzoff argued that time spent watching screens displaces speech that babies need, since infants have limited waking hours (There are only a fixed number of hours that young babies are awake and alert. If the 'alert time' is spent in front of DVDs and TV instead of with people speaking in 'parentese').
- **Publication:** The paper appeared in the Journal of Pediatrics, with citation details listed as 151(4), 364-368 (Zimmerman, F. J., Christakis, D. A., & Meltzoff, A. N. (2007). Associations between Media Viewing and Language Development in Children Under Age 2 Years. The Journal of Pediatrics, 151(4), 364-368.).

Caveats: the findings are correlational, and the study's design does not establish causation. Some outlets, including Time, described the effect as "learned" or "knew" fewer words, which blurs the measure, since the study relied on parental reports of comprehension. I did not retrieve the paper itself, so the exact statistical details should be checked against the original article.

result
Yes, this is Adolph et al. (2012), "How Do You Learn to Walk? Thousands of Steps and Dozens of Falls Per Day," *Psychological Science*, 23(11), 1387–1394.

The abstract is longer than the 125-character limit for quotes, so I've split it into consecutive quoted segments:

- "A century of research has described the development of walking based on periodic gait over a straight, uniform path."
- "The current study provides the first corpus of natural infant locomotion based on spontaneous activity during free play."
- "Locomotor experience was immense: 12- to 19-month-olds averaged 2368 steps and fell 17 times/hour."
- "Novice walkers traveled farther faster than expert crawlers, but fall rates were comparable,"
- "suggesting that increased efficiency without increased cost motivates expert crawlers to transition to walking."
- "After walking onset, natural locomotion dramatically improved:"
- "Infants took more steps, traveled farther distances, and fell less."
- "Walking was distributed in short bouts with variable paths"
- "frequently too short or irregular to qualify as periodic gait."
- "Nonetheless, measures of periodic gait and natural locomotion were correlated,"
- "indicating that better walkers spontaneously walk more and fall less."
- "Immense amounts of time-distributed, variable practice constitute the natural practice regimen for learning to walk."

**Steps per day:** The article gives no measured daily figure. In the Discussion, the authors estimate that multiplying the hourly rates by 6 hours (about half a waking day) yields roughly 14,000 steps, 46 football fields of distance, and 100 falls per day. That sentence exceeds 125 characters, so I've paraphrased it rather than quoting it.

**Distance (701 m / football fields):** The Discussion states: "travels 701 m—the length of 7.7 American football fields—and falls 17 times per hour." Note that this is an hourly figure, not a daily one. The Results give the same value as 701.2 m/hour.

**Abstract (excerpt):** The full abstract exceeds the 125-character quote limit, so here is one sentence from it:

> "Between 9 and 10 mo of age, infants show phonetic learning from live, but not prerecorded, exposure to a foreign language,"

**Key details**

- **Infants per group:** Experiment 1 had 16 infants in the Mandarin live-exposure group and 16 in the English control group. Experiment 2 had 16 in the audiovisual (AV) group and 16 in the audio-only (A) group. Some infants did not complete testing.
- **Exposure hours:** Each infant had 12 sessions of 25 minutes, or 300 minutes, which is about 5 hours.
- **Age:** Exposure began at about 9 months (mean 9.3 months in Experiment 1).
- **Results:**
  - *Live:* Mandarin-exposed infants reversed the typical decline in foreign-language perception, scoring 65.7% versus 56.7% for English controls.
  - *AV and A:* Neither group showed learning. Their scores did not differ from the English control group.
- **Taiwanese comparison:** American infants exposed to live Mandarin performed statistically equivalent to Taiwanese infants who had heard Mandarin their whole lives, t(57) = 0.67, P > 0.10.

Search results support the broad picture, with a few details to check.

- **Hart and Risley (1995) gap:** Critics say the 30 million figure is likely an exaggeration. One NPR/KQED piece says the gap was about 4 million by the time the children turned 4, not 30 million by age 3, and critics point to an observer effect in the original data collection (the low-income families in their sample may have been intimidated into silence by the presence of a researcher).

- **Sperry, Sperry & Miller:** Sources describe it as a 'failed replication' of the Hart and Risley 30 million word gap study. Its main finding was that no meaningful differences between the poor, working-class and middle-class communities in the number of words spoken by the primary caregiver appeared. One blog post argues the study is so different from Hart & Risley's investigation that I think replication is the wrong description.

- **Gilkerson et al. (2017), LENA:** This study used 329 families, nearly 8 times more, and 49,765 hours of recording, from children 2 months to age 4. Its conclusion was that the gap was about 4 million by the time the children turned 4, not 30 million by age 3. A LENA researcher also said that only if you compared the most talkative 2 percent with quietest 2 percent of families did you get a gap nearly as wide as Hart and Risley's.

**Discrepancies to check:**
- **Year of Sperry:** Your query says 2019, but one source calls it a 2018 article in *Child Development* (Sperry, Sperry, & Miller (2018)), and an NPR-based piece from June 2018 calls it "just-published." The published version may be dated 2019, so verify the citation.
- **"42 families":** The results tie the 42 figure to the Sperry study, not to Hart and Risley. One source says the Sperry study involved 42 children aged 18 to 48 months, while another says it included 42 families as well. None of the results confirm the Hart and Risley sample size, so I couldn't verify the 42-family figure for their 1995 work.
- **Gilkerson vs. Sperry comparison:** Sources note that Sperry's sample was small compared to LENA's, with one saying the LENA study collected more than the Hart and Sperry investigations combined.

The search surfaced the 2020 PNAS paper's citation and secondary coverage of its findings, but not the paper's abstract or full results tables, so the exact statistics below come from a university press release and should be checked against the paper.

- **Citation:** Ferjan Ramírez, Lytle, and Kuhl (2020), "Parent Coaching Increases Conversational Turns and Advances Infant Language Development," PNAS 117(7), 3484–3491 (doi 10.1073/pnas.1921653117). Parent Coaching Increases Conversational Turns and Advances Infant Language Development. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America, 117(7), 3484–3491.
- **Design:** Families wore LENA recorders at four points, when children were 6, 10, 14, and 18 months old (The families used LENA technology four times — when their children were 6, 10, 14, and 18 months old.). Coaching covered parentese and conversational turn-taking.
- **Vocabulary at 18 months:** Parent surveys put the coached group at about 100 words versus about 60 in the control group (Parent surveys estimated that the children's 18-month vocabulary averaged around 100 words among children of coached families, compared to 60 words among children in the control group.). The same release says coached children produced real words at almost twice the frequency of children whose parents were in the control group.
- **Mechanism:** Coaching increased parentese and infant vocalizations, and those gains kept growing after sessions ended (parent coaching resulted in an increased use of parentese and infant vocalizations that continued to grow after the end of the parent coaching sessions.). Turn-taking and child vocalizations jumped between 14 and 18 months (Between 14- and 18-months, coached families showed a drastic jump in conversational turn-taking and child vocalizations.).
- **Related work:** An earlier randomized trial by the same group (Ferjan Ramírez, Lytle, Fish, Kuhl, 2018, Developmental Science) tested coaching at 6 and 10 months with outcomes at 14 months (Parent coaching at 6 and 10 months improves language outcomes at 14 months: A randomized controlled trial.). A 2023 follow-up by Huber et al. reports that Parent coaching from 6 to 18 months improves child language outcomes through 30 months of age.

I did not confirm the 2020 paper's exact sample size, statistical tests, or effect sizes. The 6-month start for the 2020 coaching arm also needs checking against the paper itself.

The refund and the 2010 study are both well documented in these results, with one citation discrepancy noted below.

**Refund:** Disney offered $15.99 back on up to four Baby Einstein DVDs per household. The offer covered purchases made between June 5, 2004 and early September 2009. Sources give the end date as September 4 or 5. The New York Times reported it, though the University of Washington piece says the story appears to have been triggered by a press release from the Campaign for a Commercial-free Childhood and that Disney called the press release a publicity stunt and said the money-back offer was its standard policy. The campaign had also threatened a class-action lawsuit for unfair and deceptive practices unless Disney agreed to refund the full purchase price to all who bought the videos since 2004.

**Study:** DeLoache et al. (2010), "Do Babies Learn From Baby Media?", Psychological Science. The researchers recruited 96 families with children between 12 and 18 months of age to participate in a month-long study. Children who watched the DVD did not learn any more words from their monthlong exposure to it than did a control group. The best outcome came from a group where parents taught the target words during everyday activities, not from video. The study also found that parents who liked the DVD tended to overestimate how much their children had learned from it.

**Citation caveat:** The search results give inconsistent volume and page numbers for the paper. Most list Psychological Science 21(11), pp. 1570–1574. One lists pp. 2170–4, and one lists "150-1574," which appears to be a typo. Verify the citation before using it.

The search confirmed the core Ganger and Brent (2004, *Developmental Psychology*) finding, but not every detail in your query.

- **Sample and method:** The study used longitudinal data from 38 children and compared a logistic function, which has an inflection point and would indicate a spurt, against a quadratic function without one (Using longitudinal data from 38 children, they modeled the rate of word learning with two functions).
- **Count:** The abstract says just 5 children had a better logistic fit, which indicated that these children underwent a spurt. That is about 13% of 38, not one in five. A secondary source describes the figure as a fifth of all children (N=38), so your "1 in 5" likely comes from that paraphrase. Cite the abstract's 5 of 38 if you need precision.
- **First words:** The paper states that typically developing children utter their first words between 8 and 14 months of age. This conflicts with the "12 months" figure in your query, which I could not verify.
- **Not found:** Nothing in the results covered the 50-word threshold at 18 months, or Oller's canonical babbling window of 6–10 months. Those would need separate searches.

The search confirmed the Colonnesi meta-analysis but returned nothing on Tomasello, Carpenter & Liszkowski (2007), so I can't speak to that paper from these results.

**Colonnesi et al. (2010), Developmental Review:** The meta-analysis covered 25 studies, and its authors concluded that pointing is a key joint-attention behavior tied to language acquisition. The results showed both a concurrent (r = .52) and a longitudinal (r = .35) relation between pointing and language development. The effect grew with age: the largest effect sizes were observed when pointing was measured when infants were 15 months and older. Pointing with a declarative motive was linked to language, but imperative pointing was not. Only three out of 25 integrated studies had analyzed imperative pointing, and the results were inconclusive.

**A later challenge:** Kirk et al. (2022), in Developmental Review, re-analyzed the field with a multivariate approach and found a weaker link. They found a significant but small overall effect size of r = 0.20. They also noted that pointing assessed after 18 months of age and pointing measured independent of speech showed stronger effects in moderator analyses.

The Tomasello et al. (2007) paper on infant pointing at 12 months was not in the results. A later source cites it for the theory of why infants point, but it doesn't summarize the paper's findings.

The search covered the Kovács and Mehler 2009 PNAS study but returned nothing on the Byers-Heinlein, Burns, and Werker 2010 paper, so that half of your request is still unverified.

**Kovács & Mehler (2009, PNAS), "Cognitive gains in 7-month-old bilingual infants"**
- Three eye-tracking studies compared infants raised with two languages from birth against matched monolinguals. The bilingual infants showed stronger cognitive control. In 3 eye-tracking studies we show that 7-month-old infants, raised with 2 languages from birth, display improved cognitive control abilities compared with matched monolinguals.
- In the key task, both groups learned to anticipate a reward on one side of a screen after a cue. Only the bilingual infants could then shift their anticipatory looks when the reward moved to the opposite side. Whereas both monolinguals and bilinguals learned to respond to a speech or visual cue to anticipate a reward on one side of a screen, only bilinguals succeeded in redirecting their anticipatory looks when the cue began signaling the reward on the opposite side.
- Later work has questioned the effect. A close multi-center exact replication of Experiment 2 is reported in Developmental Science (2023), and the authors describe the results as highlighting the importance of multicenter replications. Robustness of the cognitive gains in 7-month-old bilingual infants: A close multi-center replication of Kovács and Mehler (2009) A separate review notes that several recent studies have not precisely replicated the infant bilingual advantage as initially reported.

**Byers-Heinlein, Burns & Werker (2010, Psychological Science)**
No results were returned for this paper, so I cannot confirm its findings on newborns of bilingual mothers (Tagalog-English) from these results. A follow-up search with the paper's title or journal would be needed.

The Impact of Encouraging Infants to Gesture on Infant Language and Maternal Mind-Mindedness","url":"https://openaccess.bilgi.edu.tr/items/5bbfdcee-e498-4a6b-b998-57ac2ab1f7bf/full"},{"title":"dc.contributor.author | Kirk, Elizabeth | |","url":"https://uhra.herts.ac.uk/handle/2299/10317?show=full"},{"title":"To Sign or Not to Sign? The Impact of Encouraging Infants to Gesture on Infant Language and Maternal Mind-Mindedness","url":"https://loan.bilgi.edu.tr/items/5bbfdcee-e498-4a6b-b998-57ac2ab1f7bf/full"},{"title":"ConnectABILITY Homepage","url":"https://connectability.ca/?p=5864"},{"title":"does baby sign make a difference","url":"https://hanen.org/SiteAssets/Helpful-Info/Articles/does-baby-sign-make-a-difference.aspx"}]

The 2013 study is Kirk, Howlett, Pine and Fletcher, "To Sign or Not to Sign?", published in *Child Development* (vol. 84, no. 2, pp. 574–590). Infants exposed to gesture did not differ from control conditions on language outcomes, so the authors found no evidence that baby sign speeds up language development.

Design: The study was described as the first randomized control trial of the effects of encouraging symbolic gesture (or "baby sign") on infant language, following 40 infants from age 8 months to 20 months. Half of the mothers were trained to model a target set of gestures to their infants. A third group interacted normally, with no focus on signs or target words, according to a Hanen Centre summary of the paper (the mothers in each group were instructed to either: model signs (while also saying the corresponding word), model target words (but no signs), or interact as they normally would).

Secondary findings: The authors reported that mothers in the gesture training conditions were more responsive to their infants' nonverbal cues and encouraged more independent action by their infant. The Hanen summary also noted that the babies in the signing group learned some of the signs (ranging between two to seventeen signs), but they didn't learn to say any more target words than children in the other two groups.

Links: [{"title":"Acquiring a Single New Word","url":"https://languagelog.ldc.upenn.edu/myl/Acquiring_a_Single_New_Word.pdf"},{"title":"Carey. 2011. Beyond Fast Mapping","url":"https://www.harvardlds.org/wp-content/uploads/2018/05/Carey.-2011.-Beyond-Fast-Mapping.pdf"},{"title":"hull-repository.worktribe.com","url":"https://hull-repository.worktribe.com/OutputFile/372536"},{"title":"www.frontiersin.org","url":"https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/human-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnhum.2019.00304/pdf"},{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3362040"},{"title":"lognostics.co.uk","url":"https://www.lognostics.co.uk/vlibrary/meara1987.pdf"},{"title":"Fast mapping in lexical development revised%20%281%29","url":"https://reading-9.eprints-hosting.org/37152/1/Fast_mapping_in_lexical_development-revised%20%281%29.pdf"},{"title":"pubannotation.org","url":"https://pubannotation.org/docs/sourcedb/PMC/sourceid/6761327"},{"title":"alenazi vol10","url":"https://research.ncl.ac.uk/media/sites/researchwebsites/arecls/alenazi_vol10.pdf"},{"title":"www.frontiersin.org","url":"https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/human-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnhum.2019.00304/epub"}]

The search turned up the 1978 paper and several secondary sources describing its design and findings. The paper itself, "Acquiring a single new word" (Carey & Bartlett, *Papers and Reports on Child Language Development*, vol. 15, pp. 17–29), appears only as a reference, not as full text.

- **Design:** A teacher introduced "chromium" during snack setup by contrasting two trays, one olive and one red. In their experiment, Carey and Bartlett presented 3–4 year-old children with two trays, one of which was red and the other one olive, and asked to bring them the "chromium" tray.
- **Results:** Children picked the olive tray. Their retention was tested a week later, when they picked the "chromium" tray among six trays, which demonstrated long-term retention of the representation of the newly learned word.
- **Fast mapping:** The term originates here. Most of mapping definitions in the field of child language acquisition are based on the classical definition of Carey and Bartlett (1978), which describes rapid creation of lexical representations for unfamiliar words.
- **Pilot and follow-up:** The pilot used fourteen children at a Rockefeller University preschool. Later accounts report that after four months, many children had only partly reorganized their color vocabularies, with two patterns of partial knowledge. One group treated "chromium" as a synonym of green or brown, and the other remembered that olive had its own name but not what it was.
- **Replication:** Carey uses this data to argue that learning new words involves a two-stage process. The first stage is fast mapping, which places a word roughly in the right semantic area. The second is slower and fills in the precise meaning.

Sources: the Language Log PDF of the paper (languagelog.ldc.upenn.edu), Carey's 2011 "Beyond Fast Mapping" (harvardlds.org), and the Frontiers in Human Neuroscience review (frontiersin.org). The Frontiers and PMC sources give the 3–4 year-old sample. The Language Log text gives the pilot size of fourteen.

- **Sample and design:** The study followed English-learning infants (n = 48) from 18 to 24 months, tracking spoken language processing over that period.

Links: [{"title":"ASA 124th Meeting New Orleans 1992 October","url":"https://auditory.org/asamtgs/asa92nwo/1aSP/1aSP2.html"},{"title":"There’s more to ‘baby talk’ than meets the ear, UW researchers show","url":"https://magazine.washington.edu/theres-more-to-baby-talk-than-meets-the-ear-uw-researchers-show"},{"title":"2000 LottoKluenderHolt","url":"https://labs.la.utexas.edu/holtlab/files/2024/07/2000_LottoKluenderHolt.pdf"},{"title":"p13.4 558","url":"https://www.coli.uni-saarland.de/Phonetics/icphs/ICPhS1995/13_ICPhS_1995_Vol_4/p13.4_558.pdf"},{"title":"Infants perceive language sounds differently by age 6 months","url":"https://scienceblogs.com/cognitivedaily/2007/10/03/infants-perceive-language-soun"},{"title":"www.bas.uni-muenchen.de","url":"https://www.bas.uni-muenchen.de/~jmh/lehre/sem/ws1617/S21.pdf"},{"title":"link.springer.com","url":"https://link.springer.com/article/10.3758/BF03212057"},{"title":"brucehayes.org","url":"https://brucehayes.org/251English/Readings/GuentherGjaja1996.pdf"},{"title":"1991%20Kuhl%20Perc.%20 %20Psycho","url":"https://ilabs.uw.edu/sites/default/files/1991%20Kuhl%20Perc.%20_%20Psycho.pdf"}]

The search confirmed the paper: Kuhl, Williams, Lacerda, Stevens, and Lindblom (1992), "Linguistic experience alters phonetic perception in infants by 6 months of age," in *Science* 255, 606–608. A 2007 Cognitive Daily post lists the citation and describes the design: 64 six-month-old babies: 32 from Sweden and 32 from the U.S.

The study tested two vowel sounds. Half of each group was tested on the common English "ee" sound, which is not found in Swedish, and half were tested on the "eu" sound, which is ordinary in Swedish but not English. The results mirrored each other across groups. American infants ignored normal variations from the prototype of the "ee" sound, yet still distinguished such variations in the "eu" sound. The exact opposite was true for the Swedish infants.

The "perceptual magnet" concept itself predates this paper. Kuhl's 1991 *Perception & Psychophysics* paper introduced it, and the prototype of the category functioned like a perceptual magnet for other category members; it assimilated neighboring stimuli, effectively pulling them toward the prototype. A later ASA abstract notes that the 1992 study showed that Swedish infants do not show a magnet effect for phoneme categories that are not present in their native language.

WebFetch{"url":"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistical_learning_in_language_acquisition","prompt":"Descri
input
{
  "url": "https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistical_learning_in_language_acquisition",
  "prompt": "Describe Saffran, Aslin & Newport 1996: number of infants, age, exposure duration (2 minutes), stimulus (4 trisyllabic nonsense words), transitional probabilities (1.0 within words vs 0.33 across), and result (looking time to part-words vs words). Quote relevant sentences."
}
result
**Saffran, Aslin & Newport (1996)**

- **Participants:** 8-month-old infants. The article does not state how many infants took part.
- **Exposure:** Two minutes of a continuous speech stream. The article says, "After exposure to the speech streams for two minutes."
- **Stimuli:** Four nonsense words, each three syllables long, delivered in a monotone with no pauses or intonation cues to word boundaries. The article describes the streams as composed of "four three-syllable "pseudowords" that were repeated randomly."
- **Transitional probabilities:** Within a word, the probability of one syllable following another was 1.0. Between words, it was much lower: after one word, any of three words could follow, so a given syllable after *ku* had only about a 33% chance.
- **Results:** In the first test, infants listened longer to nonwords (novel syllable orders) than to words from the grammar. A follow-up test compared words with part-words, which had been heard during training but spanned word boundaries. Infants again showed longer listening times to the novel part-words, which the authors took as evidence that they had learned the statistical likelihood of syllable sequences, not just their order.

The search confirmed the study's core design and the "30 million word gap," but not the exact per-hour figures you listed.

- **Design:** They eventually chose 42 families at four levels of income and education, from "welfare" to "professional class." Visits began when the babies were 7 to 9 months old, with researchers visiting for one hour, once a month, for 2 1/2 years.
- **Per-hour gap:** One source says that between professional and welfare parents, there was a difference of almost 300 words spoken per hour. I did not find the 2,153 or 616 figures in these results, so I can't confirm them.
- **Age 3:** The recorded vocabularies of children from the professional families were larger than those of the parents in the welfare families by age 3.
- **Word totals:** Projected to age four, the sources give almost 45 million words for a professional-family child versus just 13 million for a welfare-family child. One secondary source instead gives about 15 million for welfare children, so the welfare figure varies by source.
- **Later criticism:** An EdWeek article argues that the "30 million-word" gap is arguably the most famous but least significant part of a landmark study, suggesting the quality of conversation matters more than word count.

Links: [{"title":"Bilingual Babies: The Roots of Bilingualism in Newborns","url":"https://psychologicalscience.org/?p=30707"},{"title":"byers heinlein burns werker10","url":"https://www.utdallas.edu/~assmann/hcs6367/byers_heinlein_burns_werker10.pdf"},{"title":"Logo de l'OEP","url":"https://www.observatoireplurilinguisme.eu/de/themenbereiche/bildung-und-forschung/88888982-acquisition-des-langues/3038-the-roots-of-bilingualism-in-newborns-assoc-for-psychological-science"},{"title":"Bilingual Babies: The Roots of Bilingualism in Newborns","url":"https://www.psychologicalscience.org/news/releases/bilingual-babies-the-roots-of-bilingualism-in-newborns.html"},{"title":"www.sciencedaily.com","url":"https://www.sciencedaily.com/releases/2010/02/100216142330.htm"},{"title":"bilingual babies the roots of bilingualism in newborns 2","url":"https://www.psychologicalscience.org/observer/bilingual-babies-the-roots-of-bilingualism-in-newborns-2"},{"title":"www.psychologicalscience.org","url":"https://www.psychologicalscience.org/?p=35129"},{"title":"- Library Home /","url":"https://open.library.ubc.ca/collections/24/items/1.0069728"},{"title":"Psychological Science","url":"https://www.psychologicalscience.org/journals/psychological-science/0956797609360758/"}]

The 2010 study by Byers-Heinlein, Burns, and Werker is in *Psychological Science* (21(3), 343–348, DOI 10.1177/0956797609360758). The search results support these points:

- **Design:** The researchers compared English monolingual newborns with Tagalog-English bilingual newborns, using the high-amplitude sucking-preference procedure to measure language preference. Two groups of newborns were tested in these experiments: English monolinguals (whose mothers spoke only English during pregnancy) and Tagalog-English bilinguals (whose mothers spoke both Tagalog, a language spoken in the Philippines, and English regularly during pregnancy).
- **Main finding:** Monolingual newborns favored English, while bilingual newborns did not. Newborns whose mothers spoke only English during pregnancy showed a robust preference for English. In contrast, newborns whose mothers spoke both English and Tagalog regularly during pregnancy showed equal preference for both languages.
- **Mixed-language group:** A group of newborns whose mothers had spoken both Chinese and English showed an intermediate pattern of preference for Tagalog over English.
- **Discrimination:** Preference for two languages does not mean the infants confuse them. Both English monolingual newborns and Tagalog-English bilingual newborns could discriminate English from Tagalog.
- **Interpretation:** The authors argue that prenatal exposure may prepare infants to learn both native languages. Prenatal bilingual exposure may prepare bilingual infants to listen to and learn both of their native languages.

The abstract and press coverage agree on these results. The sources I found do not give sample sizes, so I can't confirm those from this search.

The search did not return the specific median values you asked for (productive vocabulary at 12, 18, and 24 months, comprehension at 12 months, or the 50-word milestone). It did confirm some background on the 1994 monograph and its instruments.

- **Source confirmed:** The monograph is Fenson et al. (1994), "Variability in early communicative development," Monographs of the Society for Research in Child Development, 59 (5, Serial No. 242).
- **Age ranges:** The Words and Gestures form is for ages 8 to 18 months, and the Words and Sentences form is for children 16 to 30 months old. The 24-month data would therefore come from the Words and Sentences form.
- **Sample:** One source reports a Words and Sentences sample of n= 1130 for children aged 16 to 30 months, though this is a secondary summary, not the monograph itself.
- **Norm revisions:** The norms were later updated. Users should check whether they need the original 1994 medians or the revised ones.

To get the exact medians and the 50-word milestone figures, the next step is to open the monograph's norm tables directly or the CDI User's Guide and Technical Manual (1993/2003 editions).

Links: [{"title":"sciencedaily.com","url":"https://sciencedaily.com/releases/2009/11/091105092607.htm"},{"title":"Help Privacy Policy Disclaimer","url":"https://pure.mpg.de/pubman/item/item_725001_4"},{"title":"idw – Informationsdienst Wissenschaft","url":"https://idw-online.de/-BTr2AA"},{"title":"idw – Informationsdienst Wissenschaft","url":"https://idw-online.de/en/news342770"},{"title":"dri.ipb.ac.id","url":"https://dri.ipb.ac.id/?p=2245"},{"title":"idw – Informationsdienst Wissenschaft","url":"https://idw-online.de/de/news342770"},{"title":"Native wails","url":"https://languagelog.ldc.upenn.edu/nll/?p=1869"},{"title":"baby accent","url":"https://www.disabled-world.com/news/offbeat/baby-accent.php"},{"title":"Can you tell the language of the mother from her baby’s cry?","url":"https://cognitionandculture.net/blogs/nicolas-claidires-blog/can-you-tell-the-language-of-the-mother-from-her-babys-cry"},{"title":"Can you tell the language of the mother from her baby’s cry?","url":"https://cognitionandculture.net/blogs/nicolas-claidires-blog/can-you-tell-the-language-of-the-mother-from-her-babys-cry/index.html"}]

The study is confirmed: Mampe, Friederici, Christophe and Wermke (2009), "Newborns' Cry Melody Is Shaped by Their Native Language," *Current Biology* 19(23), pp. 1994–1997 (Mampe, B., Friederici, A. D., Christophe, A., & Wermke, K. (2009). Newborns' cry melody is shaped by their native language. Current Biology, 19(23), 1994-1997.).

**Sample and design:** The researchers recorded and analyzed cries from 30 newborns from French-speaking families and 30 from German-speaking families. Sources differ on the age: one says three to five days old, while another gives two to five days old. A separate summary notes the analysis covered 1254 cries (selected from 2500 recordings).

**Findings:** French newborns' cries tended to rise in pitch, while German newborns' cries tended to fall, and the authors tied this to the intonation differences between the two languages (French newborns tend to cry with a rising melody contour, whereas German newborns seem to prefer a falling melody contour. Those patterns are consistent with characteristic differences between the two languages). The effect was group-level: babies from both groups produced cries with very different melodies, but on average there was a significant difference between the German and French groups.

**Interpretation:** The authors argue the effect appears earlier than expected. Surrounding language was thought to affect sound production much later, but the researchers now find that this is not so. Their explanation is that imitation of melody contour relies on well-coordinated respiratory-laryngeal mechanisms and is not constrained by articulatory immaturity.

**Caveats:** Coverage in the press was often broader than the paper. A Language Log post opened by contrasting headlines such as "Newborns cry in their native language" with the actual paper, and it was skeptical of journalistic generalizations. I did not verify the full text of that critique.

- **Citation confirmed:** A UBC student wiki lists the reference as Thelen, E. (1986), "Treadmill-elicited stepping in seven-month-old infants," *Child Development* ([wiki.ubc.ca](https://wiki.ubc.ca/Course:KIN355/2020_Projects/Stepping_Reflex)). - **Later work built on it:** A 2021 *Frontiers in Psychology* paper names Thelen's 1986 study as pioneering work. It notes that later treadmill training research found it can speed the onset of independent walking in infants with motor delays and improve step coordination ([frontiersin.org](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2021.665306/pdf)).
- **Related seven-month-old work:** A review of infant limb coordination describes a split-belt treadmill study in which seven-month-old infants, held upright, adjusted both legs to keep an alternating pattern when the belts ran at different speeds. That study is cited as Thelen et al. (1987) ([pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2278628)).

For the original abstract or full text, I'd suggest checking the journal's site (Wiley, via the Society for Research in Child Development) or a library database.

The search didn't return the StatPearls passages you named for rooting or stepping, and the sources disagree on several timelines. Here is what came back:

- **Moro:** One StatPearls passage says the reflex develops by 28 weeks gestation and disappears by six months. A pediatric education source gives a different window, saying it disappears around 3-4 months but normal up until 6 months.
- **Palmar grasp:** StatPearls says it develops by 28 weeks gestation and disappears by six months. Another source gives a similar window, placing its disappearance by 6 months.
- **Stepping:** The pediatric education source places its disappearance around 2-3 months. This is not from StatPearls.
- **Babinski:** The sources differ. Lecturio says it disappears by 12 months of age, while the pediatric education source says disappears by 1-2 years. The StatPearls passage I found describes the stimulus and response but gives no disappearance age.
- **Rooting (4 months) and ATNR (6 months):** I found no StatPearls or other source confirming the 4-month rooting figure. A nursing continuing-education resource says the asymmetric tonic neck reflex disappears by six months of age, which matches your figure.

Several sources were low quality (a flashcard site, a blog, and a form) and I did not rely on them. A StatPearls page (27744) is the most authoritative match, but I could not confirm the full ages for each reflex there. Checking the full StatPearls article directly would be the next step before citing these ages.

The search didn't turn up the 1940 *Journal of Genetic Psychology* article itself, and it didn't give walking-onset ages in months.

- **Related 1940 study:** A 1940 Dennis paper in the *Journal of Social Psychology* compared 41 Navajo and Hopi babies with White American infants up to one year. He found that the cradleboard did not slow motor development, contrary to popular belief.
- **Hopi child book:** Dennis's 1940 book *The Hopi Child* includes a section on the effect of cradleboarding on the development of walking. The search results don't give its figures.
- **Citation discrepancy:** A reference-list entry in one result cites the Dennis and Dennis cradling paper as 1991, in The Journal of Genetic Psychology, 152, 563-572. That date and volume don't match the 1940 date in your query, so check the original journal record before relying on either.
- **Modern work:** A 2023-era PMC study of Central Asian cradling found that cradling predicted walk onset age and the proficiency of sitting, crawling, and walking.

To get the actual walking-age figures, you'd need the article's full text, which might be on the Journal of Genetic Psychology/Taylor & Francis site or in a university library.

The search turned up the 1976 paper, but not the specific "one month earlier" figure or the term "Kokwet."

**The 1976 paper:** The article is "Environmental Effects on Motor Development: the Case of 'African Infant Precocity'," published in October 1976 in volume 18, issue 5, pages 561–567. Its abstract says 64 babies and their families were intensively studied in a rural Kenyan community to test claims by Geber and others about early motor development in African infants. The abstract reports that the Kenyan babies reached sitting and walking early by American standards, and that these skills are taught by caretakers and practised during daily routines (the motor skills of sitting and walking... are (a) specifically taught by the caretakers and (b) can be practised in the course of their usual daily routines).

- **Season two featured her work:** A June 2020 MIT CBMM article, reprinting a Huffington Post piece, says the show's second season included Saxe's infant research. It also notes that MIT's Laura Schulz was featured for her work with toddlers. The following story contains information on infant research conducted by CBMM PI Rebecca Saxe.
- **The scan:** In that segment, Saxe placed her own infant son, Percy, in an MRI scanner. The scanner was adapted to protect his hearing, and it looked at his brain functioning while he was awake. The scan used a sequence of happy and sad faces, with other images as a baseline, to find which brain areas responded to faces and scenes. She said the biggest surprise was the similarity between baby and grown-up brains, and that the face and scene regions were the same.
- **Underlying research:** Saxe's infant fMRI work predates the show. A 2017 Nature Communications study, led by Ben Deen, reported similar face and scene responses in infant brains. A 2022 follow-up scanned more than 50 infants aged two to nine months and found face-, body-, and scene-selective regions, using a new head coil designed for babies.

I did not find the exact episode title, number, or air date within *Babies*. The June 2020 article is the only source I found that places the segment in the series, so check the Netflix listing for those details.

**Main findings:**
- Babies aged 4 to 6 months showed the same face and scene preferences as adults in the regions previously identified in adults. Saxe said "Every region that we knew about in adults" with a face or scene preference showed the same preference in these babies.
- Saxe concluded the cortex "is already starting to have a bias in its function," rather than being undifferentiated.
- The researchers also saw engagement in frontal cortex, an area linked to emotion, values, and self-representation.
- They found no evidence of regions specialized for one category over all others. Charles Nelson, who was not involved in the study, described infant brains as "more multipurpose."
- Deen cautioned that the study can't establish whether these traits are innate: "We can’t strictly say that anything is innate."

result
**Abstract excerpt (quote limited to 125 characters):**
"Infants’ probability of looking away was greatest on stimulus items whose complexity (negative log probability)"

**Requested details:**

- **Ages:** The abstract refers to 7- and 8-month-olds. The methods give mean ages of 7.9 months (Experiment 1) and 7.6 months (Experiment 2).
- **Number of infants:** 42 infants were analyzed in Experiment 1 and 30 in Experiment 2, for 72 total.
- **Stimulus:** In Experiment 2, an object popped out from behind one of three boxes and then retreated. In Experiment 1, a single box revealed an object that appeared or vanished according to a set probability.
- **U-shaped finding:** Infants were most likely to look away at the extremes of complexity, both very predictable and very surprising events, rather than at moderate ones.
- **Measure:** Look-away probability was compared against complexity, defined as surprisal, the negative log probability of each event under an ideal learner model.
