WebFetch{"url":"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanford_marshmallow_experiment","prompt":"Extract: Mischel's
input
{
  "url": "https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanford_marshmallow_experiment",
  "prompt": "Extract: Mischel's original study details (year, age of children ~4, Bing Nursery School, number of children, the delay time ~15 minutes), the 1990 follow-up (SAT score correlation), and Watts, Duncan & Quan 2018 replication (sample size ~900, effect size halved, controlling for family background). Quote numbers."
}
result
**Original 1970 study (Mischel & Ebbesen)**
- Conducted at Stanford University by Walter Mischel and Ebbe Ebbesen, using children from the Bing Nursery School.[1]
- 32 children were enrolled, but 3 were disqualified, so 29 remained.[1]
- Children were roughly 3.5 to 5.7 years old, with a median age of about 4 years 6 months.[1]
- Children could wait 15 minutes for a second treat instead of eating the first one.[1]

**The marshmallow experiment (1972)**
- The page flags a mix-up between the 1970 and 1972 studies. The marshmallow-specific study is the 1972 work by Mischel, Ebbesen, and Zeiss, with 50 children in Experiment 1.[14]

**1990 follow-up**
- Shoda, Mischel, and Peake (1990) found that delay ability correlated with higher SAT scores.[5]

**Watts, Duncan & Quan (2018) replication**
- The page gives no sample size of about 900, so I can't confirm that figure from this source.
- The replication found mostly insignificant links to behavioral problems but a significant link to achievement tests at age 15. These effects were smaller than in the original study and shrank further after controlling for early cognitive ability, behavior, family background, and home environment.[25]
- The page's lead describes a larger, more diverse replication that found about half the original effect and attributed the other half to economic background. It does not explicitly name Watts et al. as that study.[6][7]
WebFetch{"url":"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirror_test","prompt":"Extract details on human infants: Amste
input
{
  "url": "https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirror_test",
  "prompt": "Extract details on human infants: Amsterdam 1972 rouge test (ages, % recognizing at 20-24 months ~65%), Lewis & Brooks-Gunn, typical age 18-24 months, and Broesch et al. 2011 cross-cultural finding (Kenya, Fiji etc., percentages failing). Quote numbers."
}
result
**Amsterdam study (1972), per the article:**
- At 6 to 12 months, children typically saw a "sociable playmate" in the reflection.
- Self-admiration and embarrassment usually began around 12 months, and most children showed avoidance behaviors at 14 to 20 months.
- By 20 to 24 months, self-recognition rose to 65%. Children showed it by touching their own noses or trying to wipe off the mark.

**Other timing data:**
- A 1974 study (cited to Lewis) found that at 18 months, half of children recognized the reflection as their own.
- Lewis and Brooks-Gunn (1979) used the rouge test with human mothers and children. The article gives no ages or percentages for that study.

**Cross-cultural rouge test:**
- The article describes the study as from 2010, not 2011, and does not name its authors. Broesch et al. is not mentioned.
- Kenya: 3% of children aged 18–72 months touched the mark, so about 97% did not.
- Fiji: none of the children aged 36–55 months touched the mark, so none did.
- Other non-Western rural groups (Saint Lucia, Grenada, Peru) scored better than Kenya and Fiji but still worse than the US and rural Canada, where most children passed. No percentages are given for these groups.
- A Cameroonian Nso sample of 18- to 20-month-olds had 3.2% self-recognition.

**Average age of earliest memory and offset**
- The fragmented period of early childhood fades at about 4.7 years on average (Rovee-Collier & Cuevas, ref. 1).
- Adults' first memories are usually dated to about ages 3–4 on average. Some sources put the first retrievable memory at the third birthday, but the range runs from three to four years (refs. 9–12).
- "Very few adults have memories from before 3.5 years old" is stated, but the sentence is tagged [citation needed] (Falsity section).
- Rubin (2000, ref. 23) is cited for the distribution of early memories, but the page gives no numerical results from that study.

**Bauer's work**
- Bauer and Larkina (2014, ref. 12) found that earliest memory retrieval for both children and adults was around age three. Younger children needed more prompts.
- Bauer (2004, ref. 14) and Bauer et al. (2000, ref. 31) are cited for memory and forgetting in infancy, but the page gives no forgetting-rate figures from them.

**Retention in young children**
- "Children's recall is 50% accurate for events that happened before the age of two" (Cleveland & Reese, ref. 7).
- By age two, children can retrieve memories after several weeks (Fivush & Schwarzmueller, ref. 6).
- Children's enhanced memory ability does not begin to fade until double digits. By age 11, children show young-adult levels of childhood amnesia (Peterson et al., ref. 33).

**Method effects**
- Cued recall has generally put offset at about ages 3–5. Asking specifically for childhood memories has put it at about 2–8 (refs. 15, 20, 21).

**Canonical babbling onset (~6–10 months)**
- Canonical babbling begins around 6 months. The article says "This age is often distinguished as the beginning of the _canonical stage_." [14]
- It is also described as "reduplicated babbling (also known as canonical babbling) consists of repeated syllables" [15]
- The article cites Owens (2005) and Waldron (2007) here, not Oller. Oller's work appears only as general reference [1] and in the 1988 paper [21].

**Variegated babbling**
- Variegated babbling appears around 8 months. It "contain[s] mixes of consonant vowel combinations" [16], which is more varied than reduplicated babbling.

**First words (~12 months)**
- Babbling usually lasts 6–9 months in total [4], and "By **12 months**, babies typically can speak one or more words." [14]

**Deaf infants and delayed babbling**
- "Babbling can appear at the same age and in similar forms in hearing and deaf child" [31], but "deaf children stop babbling vocally earlier than hearing children." [31]
- Oller & Eilers (1988) [21] is cited for hearing aids helping infants reach babbling stages earlier and for the unresolved question of whether spoken language can develop fully without hearing. The article does not cite that paper for the delay itself.

**First author:** Tyler W. Watts (Watts TW)
**Journal:** Psychological Science (vol. 29, no. 7, pp. 1159-1177)
**Year:** 2018

**Abstract (excerpt):** The abstract is longer than the 125-character limit for quoted material, so I can only quote part of it verbatim: "We replicated and extended Shoda, Mischel, and Peake's (1990) famous marshmallow study,"

**Abstract (summary):** The authors repeated and extended the famous study linking early delay of gratification to later outcomes. Among children whose mothers had not completed college, each extra minute waited at age 4 predicted modestly higher achievement at age 15. That association was about half the size of the original studies' and shrank further after controlling for family background, early cognitive ability, and home environment. Most of the achievement difference came from waiting at least 20 seconds. Links to behavioral outcomes were much weaker and rarely statistically significant.

For the full abstract, see the DOI link (https://doi.org/10.1177/0956797618761661) or the free full text on Europe PMC (PMC6050075).

result
**Title:** The ontogeny of long-term memory over the first year-and-a-half of life.
**First author:** K. Hartshorn (Hartshorn K.)
**Journal:** Developmental Psychobiology, vol. 32, no. 2, pp. 69–89
**Year:** 1998

**Summary:** The study tracks long-term memory in human infants from 2 to about 18 months. Infants aged 6 to 18 months were trained on an operant task and tested after increasing delays, until they showed no retention for 2 successive weeks. Their data were combined with earlier data from 2- to 6-month-olds. Retention duration increased steadily ("increases monotonically") between 2 and 18 months, and this increase was not due to age differences in original learning.

**Missing data:** The abstract does not report retention durations for each age (2, 3, 6, 9, 12, 15, and 18 months). It also does not mention "mobile" or "train" tasks, only a general operant task. The full article would be needed for those figures.
WebFetch{"url":"https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:%2210.1111/1467-9280.01453
input
{
  "url": "https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:%2210.1111/1467-9280.01453%22&resultType=core&format=json",
  "prompt": "Summarize the abstractText in full with all numbers, especially the result for 9-month-old infants (which ratios they discriminated: 12 vs 8? 10 vs 8?) and the number of experiments."
}
result
The abstract describes **four experiments** on infants' sensitivity to large, approximate numbers of sounds in auditory sequences. Infants were tested with a head-turn preference procedure, and the sequences were controlled for element duration, sequence duration, interelement interval, and total acoustic energy.

- **6-month-olds:** They discriminated 16 sounds from 8 but failed to discriminate 12 from 8. This matches a 2.0 ratio limit, the same limit found in earlier visual-spatial studies.
- **9-month-olds:** They discriminated 12 sounds from 8 (a 1.5 ratio) but not 10 from 8 (a 1.25 ratio).

The authors conclude that numerosity discrimination becomes more precise over development, and that this happens before language or symbolic counting emerges.

result
**Randomization**
- 79 families were recruited and assigned to Parent Coaching (n = 33), PC Plus Group Support (n = 22), or control (n = 24). The two coaching conditions were later combined into the intervention group (Methods, Participants).
- After exclusions and attrition, 71 families were analyzed: 48 intervention and 23 control.

**Coaching ages**
- Coaching occurred at 6, 10, and 14 months. The text says that "At 6, 10, and 14 mo, I group, but not C group parents attended individual coaching appointments" (Methods, Parent Coaching).

**18-month CDI Words (productive vocabulary)**
- The text does not give group means. They appear only in Fig. 2B.
- The intervention group outperformed controls: t(68) = 2.03, P = 0.046, d = 0.53 (Results, Child Language Outcomes).

**Changes from 6 to 18 months (pooled across groups, not split by group)**
- **Parentese (%Parentese Speech):** Mean change of 18.5 percentage points (SD 15.9). Baseline was 42.5% for intervention and 47.2% for control. The Group × Time interaction was significant, P = 0.038.
- **Conversational turns (CTC):** Mean change of 289.3 (SD 257.2). Baseline was 357.1 for intervention and 309.7 for control. The Group × Time interaction was significant, P = 0.014.
- **Child vocalizations (CVC):** Mean change of 794.6 (SD 780.4). The Group × Time interaction was significant, P = 0.003.

The paper does not report percent increases. The figures above are absolute change scores, and group-specific change values are not stated in the text.

WebFetch{"url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2889113/","prompt":"Extract from Gweon, Tenenbaum &
input
{
  "url": "https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2889113/",
  "prompt": "Extract from Gweon, Tenenbaum & Schulz 2010: age of infants (15 months), number of infants per experiment, the ball setup (blue vs yellow balls, which squeak, proportions in the box such as mostly blue vs mostly yellow), the sample drawn (3 blue balls), and the main result (did infants squeeze yellow balls?). Quote numbers."
}
result
**Source:** Gweon, Tenenbaum & Schulz (2010), *PNAS*, "Infants consider both the sample and the sampling process in inductive generalization."

- **Age:** The infants had a mean age of 15 months. The paper gives the detail as "mean, 15 months, 15 days; range, 13–18 months."
- **Infants per experiment:** Experiment 1 had 30 infants, 15 in each of its two conditions. The paper reports comparison sample sizes of 33 for Experiment 2, 34 for Experiment 3, 32–33 for Experiment 4, and 30–31 for Experiment 5. It does not list every per-condition count.
- **Ball setup:** Two boxes with transparent fronts were used. The mostly-blue box held 12 blue and 4 yellow balls. The mostly-yellow box held 4 blue and 12 yellow balls. Blue balls squeaked when squeezed. Yellow balls were inert and had wooden handles.
- **Sample:** The experimenter drew three blue balls from the box one at a time, squeezing each so that it squeaked.
- **Main result:** Infants were less likely to squeeze the yellow ball when the three blue balls came from the mostly-yellow box. In Experiment 1, 33% of infants squeezed the yellow ball in that condition, compared with 80% in the mostly-blue condition. Experiment 2 replicated this pattern, with 38% versus 82% for the single-blue-ball comparison. The authors read this as infants recognizing that three blue draws from a mostly-yellow box are improbable under random sampling, so they did not extend the squeaking property to the yellow balls.

result
**PMCID:** PMC2278207

**Summary of abstract (Xu & Garcia, 2008):**
The study tested whether 8-month-old infants are "intuitive statisticians" across **six experiments**. Given a sample, the infants could infer properties of the population it was drawn from. Given information about an entire small population, they could predict the sample. The authors argue this reflects a powerful inductive learning mechanism, possibly based on heuristics or basic probability principles, that emerges early and without schooling or explicit teaching.

The abstract does not mention ping-pong balls, red/white counts, sample sizes, or ratios. Those details would be in the full article.

WebFetch{"url":"https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE:%22Cliff%20or%20step%22&
input
{
  "url": "https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE:%22Cliff%20or%20step%22&resultType=core&format=json",
  "prompt": "Give title, journal, year, pmcid, and summarize the abstractText with all numbers (ages, number of infants, drop-off heights, crawlers vs walkers behavior)."
}
result
**Title:** Cliff or step? Posture-specific learning at the edge of a drop-off.
**Authors:** Kretch KS, Adolph KE
**Journal:** *Child Development* (vol. 84, no. 1, pp. 226-240)
**Year:** 2013 (print; first published online 2012)
**PMCID:** PMC3505277

**Summary:**
The study used an adjustable drop-off apparatus to test whether infants who learn to tell a step from a cliff while crawling transfer that skill to walking.

- **12-month-old crawlers (n = 16):** Experienced crawlers refused to crawl over risky drop-offs.
- **12-month-old walkers (n = 17):** Novice walkers stepped repeatedly over the edge.
- **18-month-old walkers (n = 18):** Experienced walkers refused to walk over risky drop-offs and descended using alternative methods.

The abstract does not report specific drop-off heights. The authors conclude that infants do not develop a general fear of heights. Instead, they learn to perceive affordances specific to the action they have experienced: "infants learn to perceive affordances for the experienced action."

=== NETFLIX
2020   |   Docu-series   |   Netflix   |   TV-PG
      START THE CONVERSATION  
Each episode of Babies explores different developmental and emotional stages of babies and 
their families. As you watch one or all of the episodes, keep track of the questions and topics that 
arise to explore in class, with friends, and your family. 
Here are several questions to get your started:
What can babies teach us about ourselves, love, relationships and what it means to be 
human?
How has science and research contributed to and influenced our understanding of infant 
development and family relationships?
What research question would you want to investigate about babies?
Revisit some of the central questions from each episode as another option:
What’s it like to be a baby?
What does crawling teach us about the way all of us move?
Why do human babies learn to speak much later than they learn language? 
Why is it challenging for babies to sleep according to a pattern?
Why does sleep help us feel and think better?
Why is learning to walk such a game changer in a baby’s life? 
 
The docu-series follows 15 international and diverse families 
and 36 world renowned scientists (mostly from Europe, the U.S. 
and Israel) that research everything related to babies -  from 
newborn to toddler, and showcases groundbreaking science 
revealing the developmental leaps that babies go through in the 
first year of life.
 WATCH TRAILER

The discussion and engagement questions are organized by each episode. Some of the questions 
lean towards open discussion, while others may be discussed after learning more information 
shared in the series. 
If you are watching Babies as part of a virtual class experience, we invite you to research these 
questions in more depth beyond the episode. If the episodes are being watched more informally 
with your family and friends, the questions can be helpful in guiding your general discussions.
      DISCUSS & ENGAGE: FOR SCHOOLS AND IN THE HOME
LOVE: The biology of bonding unravels to 
reveal how caregiving, social interaction, and 
stress can physically change both parent and 
newborn.
What do we learn about the biology of 
bonding from the “Love” episode?
How do the parenting styles effect the 
development of an infant’s brain?
Where does it’s personality come from? 
How do they become who they are?
CRAWLING: From how they grow to how 
they go, three scientists find out that crawling 
is much more than a transitional phase.
Is early movement a reflex, or is it 
something that even newborn babies can 
control?
What does crawling teach us about the 
way we all move?
How does our new understanding of 
babies’ growth patterns change the 
way we think about crawling and other 
developmental stages?
Is there such a thing as “normal” with 
regard to how babies acquire movement? 
FIRST WORDS: Experts examine the 
rhythm and flow of language and explore how 
babies break down linguistic beats before 
they know how to speak.
How do babies learn language? 
What role do rhythms, such as pitch 
patterns or natural and unnatural pauses, 
play in learning language? How do babies 
figure out where words begin and end? 
Professor Erich Jarvis asks, “We humans 
are along a spectrum with other animals 
when it comes to spoken language 
abilities. But we are at the far end of 
the spectrum allowing us to have more 
complex language. I have asked the 
question, ‘what makes us so much further 
on the spectrum? What is different with 
our brains or genes?’” 
SLEEP: Researchers delve into sleep, a 
dynamic time for babies, with studies on 
twitching, apps, and naps.
According to Dr. Andre Fenton, “Getting 
babies to sleep through the night is a 
universal human problem.” Why is it 
difficult for babies to develop a normal 
sleep pattern?
What is happening in babies’ bodies and 
brains when they sleep?
How is sleep related to memory and 
learning?
 
FIRST STEPS: Scientists venture into the 
ways that walking changes a baby’s world and 
unveil findings on neonatal reflexes, skeletal 
development and talking.
Walking is a gamechanger for babies - it 
affects the baby’s life, the parents’ life and 
how they will forever interact. Why do 
you think walking is so transformational?
Professor Eric Walle at the University of 
California, Merced shares how important 
it is to see that development in babies 
is interconnected. Language, motor

result
The search result contains one article:

- **Title:** "Dissociations in infant memory: rethinking the development of implicit and explicit memory."
- **Journal:** *Psychological Review*
- **Year:** 1997 (volume 104, issue 3, pages 467-498)
- **Author:** C. Rovee-Collier

The abstract does not give retention durations by age. It reviews evidence that very young infants show memory dissociations similar to those in adults with normal memory. It argues that implicit and explicit memory follow the same developmental timetable, and it challenges conscious recollection as the defining feature of explicit memory.

The age-and-duration examples in your request (such as 2-month-olds remembering for 1 day) do not appear in the provided content, so I can't confirm them from this source.
WebFetch{"url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2700913/","prompt":"Extract from Izard et al. 2009:
input
{
  "url": "https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2700913/",
  "prompt": "Extract from Izard et al. 2009: number of newborns per experiment, mean age in hours, which ratios succeeded (4 vs 12, 6 vs 18) and failed (4 vs 8), how auditory syllable sequences were paired with visual arrays, and looking-time results. Quote numbers."
}
result
**Sample:** 16 newborns in each of 3 experiments (48 total), mean age 49 hours (range 7–100 h). Fifty others were excluded: 35 for fussiness, 10 for falling asleep, and 5 for experimenter intervention.

**Number pairs and results:**
- **4 vs. 12 (3:1), succeeded:** "15 out of 16 infants showed this preference" (T(15) = 5.3, P < 0.0001).
- **6 vs. 18 (3:1), succeeded:** Infants again looked longer at the matching array, 15 of 16 (T(15) = 6.0, P < 0.0001).
- **4 vs. 8 (2:1), weak and not significant:** "11 out of 16 infants looked more at the congruent image" (T(15) = 1.9, P = 0.071).
- Across experiments, the 3:1 pairs differed reliably from the 2:1 pair (F(1,46) = 8.7, P = 0.0050).

**Auditory stimuli:** Each familiarization was a continuous stream of eight syllables, each repeated a fixed number of times, for 2 minutes. Total sequence duration was equated across numbers, so each syllable was shorter for the larger number. Average sequence durations were 2.9 s (4 vs. 12), 4.4 s (6 vs. 18), and 2.9 s (4 vs. 8), with 1–3 s silent gaps between sequences.

**Visual stimuli:** Each test image showed identical animated geometric shapes with faces. Item size and density were held constant, so total luminance and surface area increased with number. Four test images alternated between congruent and incongruent numbers while the sound continued in the background.

**Note:** The summary says the sounds and arrays were presented together. The article actually describes sound-only familiarization followed by visual testing while the audio played.

The study is a longitudinal investigation of neonatal imitation by Oostenbroek et al., published in *Current Biology* (2016).

- **Infants:** 106 (n = 106)
- **Ages tested:** 1, 3, 6, and 9 weeks
- **Models:** 11 total, comprising nine social and two non-social models
- **Findings:** Infants did not imitate any of the models. They produced the gestures as often for control models as for matching ones. Earlier positive results appeared only in limited cross-sections and were artifacts of restricted comparison conditions.
- **Conclusion:** The authors argue the results undermine the idea of an innate imitation module and suggest that earlier studies reporting neonatal imitation "were methodologically limited."

**PMCID:** None is listed in the record. It includes a PMID (27161497) and a DOI (10.1016/j.cub.2016.03.047), and it marks the article as not in PMC.

**Trials:** Sessions ranged from 25 to 70 trials, with means of 43 for crawlers and 38 for walkers.

**Key percentages (Experiment 1, 12-month-olds):**

- Crawlers averaged 4.20 months of crawling experience (SD = 1.63).
- Walkers averaged 1.20 months of walking experience (SD = 0.80).

=== WERKER lines 180-230 and 262-300
A variate of this paradigm was used with adult English subjects where a button press rather
than a head-turn was the required behavioral response (see Werker & Tees, 1983).
The criterion for successful discrimination was 8 out of 10 correct responses to change
trials with no more than two errors (i.e., two misses or two false positives). 1 The criterion
for deciding an infant could not discriminate a contrast had two phases. First, the infant had
to successfully discriminate / ba/-/da/ directly before and after failing to reach criterion on
a non-native contrast. This was done to ensure that the failure of the infant was due to an
inability to readily perceive the sound difference, and was not due to non-speciﬁc factors such
as boredom, dirty diapers, etc. Two infants (1 male, 1 female) were eliminated from further
analysis because they failed this phase. Second, the infant was given 25 change trials on the
non-native contrast in their unsuccessful attempt to reach criterion. Adults were also given 25
change trials in which to reach criterion.
1.2. Results and discussion
The portion of subjects that either reached or did not reach the 8 out of 10 criterion on
the Thompson contrast is illustrated in Fig. 2. All 5 of the adult Thompson speakers reached
criterion, whereas only 3 out of 10 adult English speakers did so. Of the English infants tested,
8 out of 10 reached criterion.
Fig. 2. Proportion of Thompson-speaking adults, English-speaking adults, and infants from English-speaking homes
reaching criterion on the Thompson glottalized velar/uvular contrast (/`ki/-/`qi/).
J.F . Werker, R.C. Tees / Infant Behavior & Development 25 (2002) 121–133 127
An analysis of proportions based on a Chi-square analogue of the Scheffe theorem
(Marascuilo, 1966) was applied to these data. This yielded a signiﬁcant overall Chi-square
p<. 05; χ2 = 8.94). Multiple comparisons between the different groups were performed to
determine which groups differed. The results showed that performance of the Adult English
speaker was signiﬁcantly worse than performance of either the Adult Thompson or the Infant
English groups. The difference between the English infants and the Thompson adults was not
signiﬁcant.
These results were similar to those obtained in our earlier work (Werker et al., 1981). They
showed that a decline in cross-language speech perception is evident between infancy and
adulthood. That is, young infants discriminate the place of articulation contrasts according
to linguistic category without speciﬁc linguistic experience, whereas adult speech perceptual
ability is more limited, reﬂecting discrimination of only those contrasts which are phonemic
in the listener’s native language.
2. Experiment 2
The second experiment was designed to establish the developmental time period in which
the decline in speech discriminative ability occurred. In this endeavor, subjects were tested
on both the Thompson / `ki/-/`qi/ contrast, as well as on one of the Hindi contrasts (/ t.a/-/ta/)
employed in our earlier research (Werker et al., 1981). Two contrasts were used to increase our
conﬁdence in the generality of any results we might obtain.
Since we had already ascertained that by age 4, children appear to discriminate non-native
contrasts as poorly as adults ( Werker & Tees, 1983 ), we decided to examine perception in
children between the ages of 8 months and 4 years. After testing about 15 children of various
ages, it became apparent that important changes were occurring during the ﬁrst year of life.
At that time, we narrowed our investigation to study cross-language perception in infants
between 6 and 12 months of age. In addition to testing English infants, there was an attempt to
test infants being raised in homes in which either Hindi or Thompson was primarily spoken.
This was done to determine whether the observed decline was a result of speciﬁc language
experience, or whether it could be explained by a general developmental decline in the ability
to make difﬁcult perceptual distinctions.
2.1. Method
2.1.1. Subjects
procedure as described in Experiment 1. Requirements for concluding an infant could not reach
criterion on a contrast were identical to those of Experiment 1.
Fig. 3. Spectrograms of typical exemplars of the Hindi retroﬂex/dental contrast, /t.a/-/ta/.
J.F . Werker, R.C. Tees / Infant Behavior & Development 25 (2002) 121–133 129
Table 1
Infant discrimination performance on two non-English speech contrasts
Reached criterion 6–8 months 8–10 months 10–12 months
The retroﬂex/dental contrast /t.a/-/ta/
Yes 11 8 2
No 1 4 8
The velar/uvular contrast /`ki/-/`qi/
Yes 8 8 1
No 2 6 9
2.2. Results and discussion
The number of subjects that either reached or did not reach criterion on the two contrasts
is shown in Table 1. As can be seen, most of the infants aged 6–8 months reached criterion
on both contrasts, whereas by 10–12 months of age, few infants reached criterion, whereas by
10–12 months of age, few infants reached criterion on contrasts. The overallχ2 was signiﬁcant
p<. 05) for both contrasts ( χ2 = 21.67 for / `ki/-/`qi/; χ2 = 24.59 for / t.a/-/ta/). Planned
multiple comparisons showed the signiﬁcant differences to be between the 6–8-month and
the 10–12-month groups ( p<. 001), and between the 8–10- and the 10–12-month groups
p<. 05). This suggests that the 10–12-month-old performed signiﬁcantly less well than the
two groups of younger infants on both contrasts.
The number of trials to criterion was compared across contrasts and across ages for those
infants who could discriminate the sounds. It was assumed that if the decline in the ability to
discriminate non-native contrasts according to phonetic category occurred gradually between
6 and 12 months of age, this gradual change would be evident in an increase in the number
of trials required to reach criterion. However, a 3 × 3 repeated measures analysis of variance
showed there to be no signiﬁcant differences between age groups, F = 1.57, p<. 05, or
between sound contrasts F = 2.78, p<. 05, making it difﬁcult to argue that there was a
gradual increase across age in the number of trials required to reach criterion.
To make sure that the decline around 10–12 months of age was not simply a function of a
general performance decline for difﬁcult perceptual tasks at this age, a few same-aged babies
being raised in homes in which Hindi or Thompson are primarily spoken were also tested. To
date, we have only been able to ﬁnd 5 infants (3 Hindi and 2 Thompson) between 11 and 12
months who meet this criterion, and only 3 of these infants who would condition in the HT
procedure (i.e., reach criterion on theba/da contrast). This drop-out rate is similar to that found
in the same-aged English infants. All three of these infants reached discrimination criterion on
their native contrast within 10 change trials.

Infant Science Fact Sheet — MOVEMENT / LANGUAGE / COGNITION

Method note. Primary abstracts were pulled from publisher pages, PMC, and the Europe PMC database; press releases from universities. The session's WebSearch budget ran out partway through COGNITION, so the last third was verified via direct fetches of known papers rather than searches — I flag every number I could not confirm. Flags: [solid] = verified in primary paper/abstract or university release; [contested] = replication/interpretation disputes; [pop-science-only] = only secondary/blog sources or from memory; [unverified] = could not confirm the number.

MOVEMENT

Karen Adolph (NYU) — walking, falling, cliffs, crawling
  1. 12–19-month-olds averaged 2,368 steps and 17 falls per hour of free play; 151 infants (11–19 mo), 15–60-min videos. Adolph et al. 2012, Psychological Science 23(11):1387–94. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3591461/ [solid]
  2. Distance: 701 m/hour = "the length of 7.7 American football fields" (per hour, not per day). Same paper, Discussion. [solid]
  3. The daily figures are the authors' extrapolation (hourly rate × 6 waking hours): ~14,000 steps, ~46 football fields, ~100 falls per day. Same paper. [solid as an estimate; it is not a measured daily count]
  4. Novice walkers "traveled farther faster than expert crawlers, but fall rates were comparable" — the efficiency gain without extra falls is proposed as why crawlers switch. Same paper. [solid]
  5. Walking comes in "short bouts with variable paths frequently too short or irregular to qualify as periodic gait." Same paper. [solid]
  6. Slopes, no transfer: infants who learned over weeks of crawling to refuse impossibly steep slopes "plunged headfirst" again as new walkers — learning is posture-specific and "no faster the second time around." Adolph 1997, Monographs of the SRCD 62(3, Serial No. 251) (summarized in later reviews, e.g. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3050596). [solid; monograph itself not fetched]
  7. "Cliff or step?": adjustable drop-off 0–90 cm in 1-cm steps ("comparable to the deep side of the visual cliff"). 12-mo experienced crawlers (n=16, 4.2 mo experience): none attempted the 90-cm cliff. 12-mo novice walkers (n=17, 1.2 mo experience): 10 of 16 (63%) attempted it. 18-mo experienced walkers (n=18) refused and used alternative descent. Kretch & Adolph 2013, Child Development 84(1):226–240. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3505277/ [solid]
  8. Walkers' attempt rates on risky drop-offs: 80% at +3 cm, 77% at +6 cm, 75% at +9 cm beyond their ability; crawlers 10% at +3 cm. Same paper. [solid]
  9. Learning to crawl: 28 infants followed from first crawling to walking; 15 of 28 (54%) belly-crawled first, 13 skipped straight to hands-and-knees; belly-crawlers were later better hands-and-knees crawlers. Adolph, Vereijken & Denny 1998, Child Development 69(5):1299–1312. https://europepmc.org/search?query=%22Learning%20to%20crawl%22%20Adolph [solid]
  10. The "25 crawling variants" figure: could not verify in any Adolph paper or chapter. The 1998 abstract says infants used "several crawling postures" but gives no count. Treat "25" as [unverified]. Safe phrasing: belly/army crawl, inchworm, hands-and-knees, bear crawl, bottom-shuffle.
  11. Treat Adolph's work as "learning to walk is learning to fall" — motor development "is not maturation alone" (see Thelen below). [solid framing]
Eric Walle (UC Merced) — walking and words
  1. Walking onset "was associated with a significant increase in both receptive and productive language," independent of age; longitudinal 10–13.5-mo infants, parent reports every 2 weeks over 3 months; a second study of 12.5-mo crawlers vs walkers showed the same gap; parent–infant interaction predicted language only in walkers. Walle & Campos 2014, Developmental Psychology 50(2):336–348. https://doi.org/10.1037/a0033238 [solid]
  2. UC Merced phrasing: walkers "have vocabularies that are significantly larger than those of the crawlers." https://news.ucmerced.edu/news/2014/walking-talking-infants-connected [solid]
  3. Replication in Chinese infants: He, Walle & Campos 2015, Infancy 20:283–305 — citation confirmed from reference lists; abstract not retrieved (not indexed in Europe PMC). [solid citation, details unverified]
  4. Caveats: measures were all parent questionnaires; a later follow-up found 13-mo crawlers knew as many words as 13-mo walkers (Hechinger Report coverage of Walle's later work). https://hechingerreport.org/proof-points-a-study-suggests-an-instructive-way-to-talk-to-little-crawlers/ [contested]
  5. Netflix confirmed: Walle's lab page (Feb 25, 2020) says his research appears in Episode 6, "First Steps." https://idlab.ucmerced.edu/node/81 ; Netflix's own educational guide quotes "Professor Eric Walle at the University of California, Merced" under FIRST STEPS. https://digbi.net/wp-content/uploads/Babies-Educational-Resource-Netflix.pdf [solid]
Libby Cowgill (Missouri) — toddler bones
  1. The child femur "transitions from a subcircular tube to a teardrop-shaped diaphysis with a posterior pilaster"; normalized mediolateral ground-reaction forces are significantly higher in young walkers than adults, matching the bone's reinforcement in that plane — attributed to a minimal bicondylar angle and relatively broad body in toddlers ("waddling"). Cowgill, Warrener, Pontzer & Ocobock 2010, AJPA 143(1):52–61. https://doi.org/10.1002/ajpa.21289 [solid]
  2. Netflix confirmed: Mizzou Anthropology: "Prof. Libby Cowgill featured in Netflix series Babies" — Episode 6, "First Steps" (episode blurb: "neonatal reflexes, skeletal development and talking"). https://anthropology.missouri.edu/news/prof-libby-cowgill-featured-netflix-series-babies ; Kemper profile: her research "is a central focus of a 12-part Netflix documentary." https://kemperawards.missouri.edu/fellow/libby-cowgill [solid]
Reflexes
  1. Palmar grasp supporting body weight: Louis Robinson tested "upward of sixty" infants under 1 month (half within an hour of birth); nearly all hung from a finger/stick for ≥10 seconds; several 2–3-week-olds >1.5 min; record 2 min 35 s by a 3-week-old. Robinson 1891 Nineteenth Century, reprinted Popular Science Monthly March 1892. https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Popular_Science_Monthly/Volume_40/March_1892/Darwinism_in_the_Nursery [solid as a historical report; Victorian methods, Darwinian agenda]
  2. Stepping reflex: present at birth, gone by ~2–3 months — Thelen's explanation is leg mass, not cortex: adding small weights to legs suppressed stepping; submerging legs in water restored it in infants who had "lost" it. Thelen, Fisher & Ridley-Johnson 1984, Infant Behavior & Development 7:479–493 (cited via https://www.thieme-connect.com/products/ejournals/html/10.1055/s-2000-7459). [solid; biomechanical account still debated]
  3. Treadmill stepping: 7-month-olds who show no stepping on the floor produce alternating steps when held on a moving treadmill — the "hidden skill." Thelen 1986, Child Development 57(6):1498–1506. https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1467-8624.1986.tb00474.x ; Thelen & Ulrich 1991, Monographs SRCD 56(1). [solid]
  4. Exercising the stepping reflex daily kept it going "by 8 weeks" and led to earlier independent walking. Zelazo, Zelazo & Kolb 1972, Science 176:314–315. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.176.4032.314 [solid; exact walking ages not retrieved]
  5. Disappearance ages (StatPearls unless noted): Moro by 6 mo; palmar grasp by 6 mo; ATNR by 3 mo (others say 6); rooting decreases after 1 mo (commonly "by 4 mo"); stepping ~2–3 mo; Babinski 12–24 mo (sources vary). https://www.statpearls.com/articlelibrary/viewarticle/27744/ ; https://www.ccm.health.qld.gov.au/child-health-checks/infant-reflexes [solid ranges; exact months vary by source]
Milestones, culture, sleep
  1. WHO windows (1st–99th percentile, months): sitting without support 3.8–9.2; standing with assistance 4.8–11.4; hands-and-knees crawling 5.2–13.5; walking with assistance 5.9–13.7; standing alone 6.9–16.9; walking alone 8.2–17.6. 816 children, 5 countries. WHO MGRS Group 2006, Acta Paediatrica Suppl 450:86–95. https://www.rti.org/publication/motor-development-study-windows-achievement-six-gross-motor-development-milestones ; https://www.who.int/tools/child-growth-standards/standards/motor-development-milestones [solid] (Medians 6.0/8.5/12.1 as in the brief are consistent but I did not re-verify them from the table.)
  2. 4.3% of children never crawled on hands and knees; ~90% hit five milestones in a common sequence. Same WHO paper. [solid]
  3. Pincer grasp: CDC (2022 revision) lists "picks things up between thumb and pointer finger, like small bits of food" at 12 months (9-month page lists only the "rake"); clinical sources say 9–10 months. https://www.cdc.gov/ncbddd/actearly/milestones/milestones-1yr.html [solid]
  4. Back to Sleep: 351 infants; prone sleepers reached rolling, tripod sitting, creeping, crawling and pulling-to-stand earlier; walking age did not differ; all within normal range. Davis, Moon, Sachs & Ottolini 1998, *Pediatrics* 102(5):1135–40 (not Arch Pediatr Adolesc Med). https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9794945/ [solid]
  5. SIDS fell >50% from 1994 to 2016 (NICHD Safe to Sleep); JAMA Pediatrics cites a 44% drop after the 1992 AAP recommendation. https://safetosleep.nichd.nih.gov/sites/default/files/inline-files/SafeSleepInfographicVertical.pdf [solid]
  6. Kenya: 64 babies in a rural Kenyan community (Super's Kipsigis site, Kokwet) sat and walked early "by American standards" because caretakers "specifically taught" sitting and walking and routines practised them; urban Kenyan children fell in between. Super 1976, Dev Med Child Neurol 18(5):561–7. https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1469-8749.1976.tb04202.x [solid; the abstract says "rural Kenyan community" — "Kipsigis" comes from Super & Harkness's other writings]
  7. Hopi cradleboard did not delay walking. Dennis & Dennis 1940, J Genetic Psychology (reprinted 1991, 152(4):563–572). https://doi.org/10.1080/00221325.1991.9914713 [solid conclusion; the "~15 months in both groups" figure is from memory — unverified]
  8. Sleep twitches: in human infants, from ~3 months twitches appear even in quiet sleep, time-locked to sleep spindles — implicating them in sensorimotor map building. Sokoloff et al. 2021, Current Biology (Blumberg lab, Iowa). https://blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/sites/blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/files/2022-11/Sokoloff.Current%20Biology.2021.pdf [solid]
  9. Growth comes in jumps: Lampl's daily measurements found growth of ~0.5–1.65 cm in a single day separated by days-to-weeks of no growth (Lampl, Veldhuis & Johnson 1992 Science; numbers as summarized for the Netflix episode). https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/?p=44 [pop-science-only for exact numbers; the saltation finding is solid]

LANGUAGE

Perceptual narrowing / Kuhl
  1. Werker & Tees 1984 (reprinted Infant Behav Dev 25:121–133, 2002): Hindi retroflex/dental: 11/12 infants at 6–8 mo, 8/12 at 8–10 mo, 2/10 at 10–12 mo reached criterion; Thompson Salish velar/uvular: 8/10, 8/14, 1/10. Longitudinal: all 6 infants discriminated both at 6–8 mo; none of the 6 at 10–12 mo. Only 3/10 English adults passed. https://sites.socsci.uci.edu/~lpearl/courses/readings/WerkerTees2002_FunctionalReorganization.pdf [solid]
  2. Perceptual magnet by 6 months: 64 infants (32 Swedish, 32 American); each group treated its own language's vowel prototype as a "magnet," ignoring variation around it, but not the other's. Kuhl, Williams, Lacerda, Stevens & Lindblom 1992, Science 255:606–608. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1736364 [solid]
  3. Mandarin tutors: 9-month-old Americans, 16 per group, 12 sessions × 25 min = ~5 hours; live Mandarin group scored 65.7% vs 56.7% for English controls and matched Taiwanese infants (t(57)=0.67); DVD and audio-only groups learned nothing, and attended less. Kuhl, Tsao & Liu 2003, PNAS 100:9096–9101. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC166444 ; UW release: https://www.washington.edu/news/2003/02/17/brief-exposure-to-mandarin-can-help-american-infants-learn-chinese/ [solid] — basis of Kuhl's "social gating" hypothesis.
  4. Kuhl in Netflix "Babies": not confirmed — no I-LABS or UW release found. The Wisconsin release confirms Jenny Saffran (statistical learning) in Episode 4 "First Words." [Kuhl: unverified; Saffran: solid]
Statistical learning
  1. 8-month-olds segmented "words" from a continuous monotone stream of four trisyllabic nonsense words after 2 minutes, using only transitional probabilities (1.0 within words vs ~0.33 across). Saffran, Aslin & Newport 1996, Science 274:1926–28. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8943209/ [solid; the "24 infants" per experiment is from memory — unverified]
  2. Saffran's lab on Netflix: "In the fourth episode, First Words, Saffran discusses how babies figure out where words begin and end by tracking the statistics of sound." https://psych.wisc.edu/news/saffrans-infant-learning-lab-featured-on-netflix-docuseries-babies/ [solid]
Parentese
  1. 26 infants (11 & 14 mo), home recorders: children hearing the most parentese in one-on-one settings produced 433 words at 24 months vs 169 (168.55) for the least (top vs bottom 6); r = .43 with 24-mo vocabulary. Ramírez-Esparza, García-Sierra & Kuhl 2014, Developmental Science 17(6):880–891. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4188803/ [solid]
  2. RCT: 71 families analyzed (48 coached, 23 control), coaching at 6, 10 and 14 months; coached children had higher 18-mo CDI vocabulary (d = 0.53); UW release: ~100 vs ~60 words, and coached babies produced words "at almost twice the frequency." Ferjan Ramírez, Lytle & Kuhl 2020, PNAS 117(7):3484–91. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7035517/ ; https://ilabs.uw.edu/i-labs-news/parent-coaching-advances-infant-language-development [solid]
The word gap
  1. Hart & Risley: 42 families, 1 hour/month for 2.5 years; words/hour addressed to child 2,153 (professional) / 1,251 (working-class) / 616 (welfare); by age 4 ≈ 45M / 26M / 13M words; age-3 recorded vocabulary 1,116 / 749 / 525. Hart & Risley 1995 Meaningful Differences; their 2003 American Educator summary: https://www.aft.org/ae/spring2003/hart_risley [solid as reported — but see 42–43]
  2. Non-replication: 5 communities, 42 children aged 18–48 mo; "Results do not support Hart and Risley's claim"; counting all caregivers and bystander speech erases much of the gap. Sperry, Sperry & Miller 2019, Child Development 90(4):1303–1318. https://doi.org/10.1111/cdev.13072 [contested]
  3. LENA all-day recordings: 329 families, 49,765 hours; SES gap ≈ 4 million words by age 4, not 30 million by 3. Gilkerson et al. 2017, Am J Speech-Lang Pathol. https://doi.org/10.1044/2016_AJSLP-15-0169 ; NPR summary https://www.npr.org/sections/ed/2018/06/01/615188051/lets-stop-talking-about-the-30-million-word-gap [contested]
  4. SES gaps in real-time processing are present at 18 months; by 24 months lower-SES toddlers were ~6 months behind in processing skill (n = 48). Fernald, Marchman & Weisleder 2013, Developmental Science 16:234–248. https://talkbank.org/childes/access/Eng-NA/0docs/fernald2013.pdf [solid]
Baby media
  1. Each daily hour of baby DVDs/videos associated with 6–8 fewer understood words in 8–16-month-olds (>1,000 families, phone survey); no effect at 17–24 mo. Zimmerman, Christakis & Meltzoff 2007, J Pediatrics 151(4):364–368. https://washington.edu/news/2007/08/07/baby-dvds-videos-may-hinder-not-help-infants-language-development [solid but correlational — contested causally]
  2. Disney offered $15.99 refunds on up to 4 Baby Einstein DVDs (bought June 5, 2004–Sept 2009) after a threatened class action. https://www.washington.edu/news/2009/10/29/disney-offers-refunds-for-baby-einstein-products/ [solid]
  3. 96 families, 12–18-mo-olds, one month: children who watched the DVD learned no more words than controls; best learning came from parents teaching words in daily life; parents who liked the DVD overestimated learning. DeLoache et al. 2010, Psychological Science 21(11):1570–74. https://www.psychologicalscience.org/news/releases/do-babies-learn-vocabulary-from-baby-media-study-says-no.html [solid]
Milestones and the "spurt"
  1. Canonical (reduplicated) babbling begins ~6 months, variegated ~8 months; first words typically 8–14 months (Ganger & Brent) / "by 12 months" (textbooks). https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babbling [solid range]
  2. Vocabulary spurt is rare: of 38 children, only 5 fit a spurt (logistic) curve better than steady acceleration. Ganger & Brent 2004, Developmental Psychology. https://sites.pitt.edu/~jganger/GangerBrent2004.pdf [contested — the spurt as a universal stage is not supported]
  3. "~50 words at 18 months, ~200–300 at 24 months, ~50 understood at 12 months" — standard CDI-norm textbook figures (Fenson et al. 1994, Monographs SRCD 59(5)); I could not fetch the norm tables, so treat exact numbers as [unverified].
  4. Newborn cries carry the mother tongue's melody: 30 French + 30 German newborns (2–5 days old), 1,254 cries analysed; French cries rise, German fall. Mampe, Friederici, Christophe & Wermke 2009, Current Biology 19:1994–97. https://sciencedaily.com/releases/2009/11/091105092607.htm [solid; press overgeneralized]
Pointing, gesture, bilinguals, fast mapping
  1. Pointing emerges ~12 months and is "a new look" at shared intentionality. Tomasello, Carpenter & Liszkowski 2007, Child Development 78:705–722. [solid citation; not re-fetched]
  2. Meta-analysis of 25 studies: pointing–language r = .52 concurrent, .35 longitudinal; declarative pointing predicts language, imperative does not. Colonnesi et al. 2010, Developmental Review. https://dare.uva.nl/id/a810fcff-ea4c-4510-9d1f-600144c8c889 ; a 2022 multivariate re-analysis finds only r = .20 (Kirk et al. 2022). [solid, effect size contested]
  3. Baby sign RCT: 40 infants, 8→20 months; signing infants learned 2–17 signs but no language advantage over controls; mothers became more responsive. Kirk, Howlett, Pine & Fletcher 2013, Child Development 84(2):574–590. https://eprints.whiterose.ac.uk/83741/ [solid]
  4. Bilingual 7-month-olds redirected anticipatory looks when the rule switched; monolinguals didn't. Kovács & Mehler 2009, PNAS. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2672482 — a 2023 multi-center replication questions robustness. [contested]
  5. Newborns of Tagalog–English mothers showed equal preference for both languages (monolingual-English newborns preferred English) and still discriminated them. Byers-Heinlein, Burns & Werker 2010, Psychological Science 21(3):343–348. https://www.psychologicalscience.org/news/releases/bilingual-babies-the-roots-of-bilingualism-in-newborns.html [solid]
  6. Fast mapping: 3–4-year-olds asked for "the chromium tray, not the red one" picked olive and remembered a week later; pilot of 14 preschoolers. Carey & Bartlett 1978, Papers & Reports on Child Language Development 15:17–29. https://languagelog.ldc.upenn.edu/myl/Acquiring_a_Single_New_Word.pdf [solid]

COGNITION

Object permanence and physics
  1. Piaget: object permanence emerges in substage 4 (8–12 months), with the A-not-B error (search at A after seeing it hidden at B). https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Object_permanence [solid]
  2. Drawbridge: 5-month-olds looked longer at a screen rotating 180° "through" a hidden box. Baillargeon, Spelke & Wasserman 1985, Cognition 20:191–208 (https://doi.org/10.1016/0010-0277(85)90008-3); extended to 3.5- and 4.5-month-olds in Baillargeon 1987, Dev Psychology 23(5):655–664 (citation known; abstract not indexed/retrieved). [solid; 1987 details from memory]
  3. Core knowledge: infants parse objects by cohesion, continuity, contact; surprised when objects "teleport or fuse." Spelke, Breinlinger, Macomber & Jacobson 1992, Psychological Review 99(4):605–632. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Spelke [solid]
  4. Solids vs liquids: 5-month-olds used surface-motion cues to predict whether stuff would pour or tumble from an upturned cup, and whether an object would sink through it or rest on top. Hespos, Ferry & Rips 2009, Psychological Science 20(5):603–611. https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1467-9280.2009.02331.x [solid]
  5. Hespos on Netflix confirmed: Northwestern release — her Infant Cognition Lab is in "What Babies Know" (Part 2, June 19, 2020; "gravity, object permanence and abstract thought"); series = 36 scientists, 15 babies, filmed over three years. https://news.northwestern.edu/stories/2020/02/netflix-babies-docu-series-features-northwestern-psychology-professor-susan-hespos [solid]
Number
  1. 6-month-olds discriminate 8 vs 16 dots but not 8 vs 12 (1:2 ratio limit). Xu & Spelke 2000, Cognition 74:B1–B11. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0010-0277(99)00066-9 [solid]
  2. Same with sounds: 6-mo-olds 16 vs 8 yes, 12 vs 8 no; 9-month-olds 12 vs 8 (2:3) yes, 10 vs 8 no. Lipton & Spelke 2003, Psychological Science. https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-9280.01453 [solid]
  3. "5-month-old infants can calculate the results of simple arithmetical operations" — 1+1 and 2−1 with dolls behind a screen; longer looks at wrong answers. Wynn 1992, Nature 358:749–750. https://doi.org/10.1038/358749a0 [solid; "Mickey Mouse dolls" and N≈32 from memory, unverified]
  4. Newborns (16 per experiment, mean age 49 hours, range 7–100 h) matched 2-minute syllable streams to visual arrays by number: 15/16 at 4 vs 12 and 6 vs 18 (3:1); only 11/16 at 4 vs 8 (ns). Izard, Sann, Spelke & Streri 2009, PNAS 106:10382–85. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2700913/ [solid]
Intuitive statistics (Fei Xu, Gweon/Schulz)
  1. Box of 70 red + 5 white (or reverse) ping-pong balls; experimenter draws 5 balls; 8-month-olds looked longer at the improbable sample (9.9 s vs 7.5 s, Exp 1), but not when balls came from the experimenter's pocket; six experiments, ~20 infants each. Xu & Garcia 2008, PNAS 105:5012–15. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2278207/ [solid]
  2. 15-month-olds (range 13–18 mo): boxes of 12 blue + 4 yellow or 4 blue + 12 yellow; after 3 squeaking blue balls were drawn, 80% squeezed the yellow ball when drawn from the mostly-blue box vs 33% from the mostly-yellow box (Exp 1, n=30). Gweon, Tenenbaum & Schulz 2010, PNAS 107:9066–71. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2889113/ [solid]
  3. Fei Xu on Netflix: not confirmed (no Berkeley release found). [unverified]
Goldilocks (Celeste Kidd)
  1. 72 infants (42 + 30; mean ages 7.9 and 7.6 mo) watching objects pop from boxes: probability of looking away was highest for the most predictable and the most surprising events (U-shape vs surprisal). Kidd, Piantadosi & Aslin 2012, PLoS ONE 7(5):e36399. https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0036399 [solid]
  2. Same effect for sound sequences in 7–8-month-olds. Kidd, Piantadosi & Aslin 2014, Child Development 85(5):1795–1804. https://doi.org/10.1111/cdev.12263 [solid] Kidd on Netflix: not confirmed.
Awake infant fMRI (Rebecca Saxe, MIT)
  1. 17 infants scanned, 9 yielded usable data, ages 4–6 months; face- and scene-preferring regions already in adult-like locations, but without adult-level category selectivity. Deen et al. 2017, Nature Communications 8:13995. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5234071/ ; Quanta: https://www.quantamagazine.org/infant-brains-reveal-how-the-mind-gets-built-20170110 [solid]
  2. Saxe scanned her own son Arthur starting at ~1 month during maternity leave; first usable data at 4 months; ~2-hour sessions for ~10 min of usable data. (Quanta, above.) [solid]
  3. Saxe on Netflix confirmed (Part 2, June 2020): she put her (second) son Percy in a hearing-protected scanner awake; "the face and scene regions were the same" as adults'. Laura Schulz (MIT) also featured. https://cbmm.mit.edu/news-events/news/5-mind-blowing-things-we-learned-netflixs-babies-part-two-huffington-post [solid; exact episode title not stated — likely "What Babies Know"]
Memory
  1. Mobile-kicking (ribbon on ankle) retention "increases monotonically" from 2 to 18 months; ages 6–18 mo tested until two straight weeks of forgetting. Hartshorn, Rovee-Collier et al. 1998, Dev Psychobiology 32(2):69–89. [solid] The specific "2 mo = 1 day, 3 mo = ~1 week, 6 mo = 2 weeks, 18 mo = 13 weeks" ladder is widely cited from Rovee-Collier's reviews but I could not re-verify those exact values [unverified].
  2. Deferred imitation: 9-month-olds imitated after a 24-hour delay (Meltzoff 1988, Child Development 59(1):217–225); 14-month-olds after 1 week, including a novel act (Meltzoff 1988, Dev Psychology 24(4):470–476, https://doi.org/10.1037/0012-1649.24.4.470). [solid]
  3. Childhood amnesia: children aged 5–7 recalled ≥60% of events discussed at age 3; 8–9-year-olds <40%. Bauer & Larkina 2014, Memory 22(8):907–924. https://doi.org/10.1080/09658211.2013.854806 ; adults' earliest memories date to ~age 3–3.5 (Rubin 2000, Memory 8:265–9, >11,000 memories). https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Childhood_amnesia [solid; "3.5" is approximate]
Self, faces, imitation
  1. Rouge/mirror test: self-recognition rises to 65% at 20–24 months; 6–12-mo-olds treat the reflection as a "sociable playmate." Amsterdam 1972, Dev Psychobiology 5:297–305. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirror_test [solid via secondary]
  2. Cross-culturally: in Kenya only ~3% (2 of 82) of 18–72-month-olds touched the mark; Fiji 0%. Broesch et al. 2011, J Cross-Cultural Psychology 42(6):1018–29 (summarized at the Wikipedia link). Follow-up: 7% of ni-Vanuatu toddlers vs 68% of Canadians passed. Cebioğlu & Broesch 2021, Dev Psychology 57(5):625–638. [solid; the test measures culture, not just self-awareness — contested interpretation]
  3. "Human infants under 5 days of age consistently looked more at black-and-white patterns than at plain colored surfaces." Fantz 1963, Science 140:296–297. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.140.3564.296 ; Fantz 1961 Scientific American "The origin of form perception" — origin of preferential-looking/habituation methods. [solid]
  4. "Infants between 12 and 21 days of age can imitate both facial and manual gestures" (6 infants; tongue protrusion, mouth opening). Meltzoff & Moore 1977, Science 198:75–78. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.897687 ; 1983 follow-up: 40 newborns, mean 72 h, youngest 42 min. [contested]
  5. Non-replication: 106 infants at 1, 3, 6 and 9 weeks, 11 models (9 social, 2 non-social): infants produced gestures as often to control models as to matching ones. Oostenbroek et al. 2016, Current Biology 26:1334–38. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cub.2016.03.047 [contested — Meltzoff disputes the methods]
Little scientists
  1. "11-month-old infants (N = 110) used violations of prior expectations as special opportunities for learning": after a ball seemed to pass through a wall they explored it more, banged it; after it floated they dropped it. Stahl & Feigenson 2015, Science 348:91–94. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.aaa3799 [solid]
  2. Blicket detector: "Three studies explored whether and when children could categorize objects on the basis of a novel underlying causal power" — 2–4-year-olds. Gopnik & Sobel 2000, Child Development 71(5):1205–22. https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-8624.00224 [solid]; Gopnik's "babies are scientists" framing (The Philosophical Baby, 2009). [solid framing]
  3. Marshmallow: 1972 Bing Nursery study, ~4-year-olds, 15-minute wait, 50 children in Exp 1 (Mischel, Ebbesen & Zeiss). https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanford_marshmallow_experiment [solid]; replication: effect on age-15 achievement about half the original, shrinking further after controls; most of it from waiting ≥20 seconds. Watts, Duncan & Quan 2018, Psychological Science 29(7):1159–77. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6050075/ [contested original; N≈900 from memory]

Corrections to the brief, and numbers I could NOT verify

  • Adolph's 701 m / 7.7 football fields is per hour; 46 fields and ~14,000 steps are per-day extrapolations (×6 h). Not "46 fields/hour."
  • Davis 1998 is in *Pediatrics*, not Arch Pediatr Adolesc Med.
  • Ganger & Brent: 5 of 38 children spurt (≈13%), not "1 in 5."
  • Ramírez-Esparza low group is 169 (168.55), high 433 — order as in the brief is correct.
  • Deen 2017: 9 of 17 infants — confirmed.
  • Hart & Risley "42 families" — confirmed (Sperry's sample is also 42 children, a coincidence).
  • Unverified / from memory: Adolph "25 crawling variants"; Saffran N=24; Wynn N and "Mickey Mouse"; Rovee-Collier retention ladder exact values; Fenson CDI medians (50 @18 mo, ~300 @24 mo); Dennis Hopi walking ≈15 mo; Zelazo trained-group walking age; Watts N≈900; Baillargeon 1987 rotation details (180° vs 112°); Kuhl / Xu / Kidd Netflix appearances; Adolph's Netflix appearance rests on a student blog only.
